On the Composition of Drugs by Kind

De compositione medicamentorum per genera · Kühn XIII 362-1058

The first complete English translation we know of. No complete translation in any modern language. Parts exist in English: G. Baker 1574 (book 3, from a French epitome), M. Marai 2023 (substantial excerpts, Warwick PhD thesis) and ten reference passages (galen_project, 2026). This is rendering A, made by a language model and not yet checked by a scholar. Greek (Kühn)

ἄλλους 563 τὰ ὑπομνήματα ταῦτα μὴ δυναμένους καλῶς χρῆσθαι τοῖς ἐγγεγραμμένοις, ἢ διὰ τὸ μὴ μεμαθηκέναι παρὰ διδασκάλου τὴν θεωρίαν τῆς ἰατρικῆς τέχνης, ἀλλὰ ὅλως ἀνεισάκτους τε καὶ ἀγυμνάστους εἶναι τὸ παράπαν ἢ διὰ τὸ κακῶς μεμαθηκέναι παραμένοντας τοῖς ἄνευ λόγου διὰ μόνης πείρας εὑρῆσθαι λέγουσι τὰ σύνθετα φάρμακα. καὶ γὰρ οὗτοι προφανῶς ἁμαρτάνουσι φιλονεικοῦντες. ἕτερον δὲ τρόπον οὐδὲν ἧττον αὐτῶν οἱ νομίζοντες οὐδὲν φάρμακον ἐξ ἐναντίων χρῆναι ταῖς δυνάμεσι. συντίθεσθαι τῶν ἁπλῶν; ἀλλὰ τούτους μὲν ἤλεγξε τὰ κατὰ τὸ πρῶτον ὑπόμνημα γεγραμμένα φάρμακα μεγάλας ἐπιδειξάμενα τὰς ἑαυτῶν δυνάμεις, ἐξ αὐτῶν τῶν τὴν σύνθεσιν ἐξ ἐναντίων ἐσχηκότων ἁπλῶν. οὐχ ἥκιστα δὲ καὶ κατὰ τὰ μέλλοντα γραφήσεσθαι μετὰ τοῦτο τὸ βιβλίον, ἐπιδείξομεν ἄριστα φάρμακα τὴν σύνθεσιν ἐξ ἐναντίων ταῖς δυνάμεσιν ἔχοντα.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἐξ αὐτῶν τῶν τὴν σύνθεσιν ἐξ ἐναντίων ἐσχηκότων ἁπλῶν

others 563 who are not able to make good use of what is written in these treatises, either because they have not learned the theory of the medical art from a teacher, but are altogether uninitiated and entirely untrained, or because they have learned badly, adhering to those who say that compound drugs have been discovered without reason, through experience alone. For these men manifestly err out of contentiousness. And in another way, no less than they, do those err who think that no drug ought to be compounded from simples that are opposite in their powers. But these latter have been refuted by the drugs recorded in the first treatise, which displayed their own great powers, derived from the very simples opposite to one another from which they had their composition. And not least also in what is to be written after this book, we shall display excellent drugs having their composition from things opposite in their powers.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἀνεισάκτους, χρῆναι ταῖς δυνάμεσι. συντίθεσθαι τῶν ἁπλῶν;

others 563, these notes, that is, falling into the hands of people who are unable to make good use of what is written in them. This may be either because they have not learned the theory of the medical art from a teacher, but are wholly uninstructed and altogether untrained, or because they have learned badly, keeping to those who say that compound drugs have been discovered without reasoning, through experience alone. For these plainly go wrong out of love of contention. In another way, and no less than they, so do those who think that no drug ought to be compounded from simples that are contrary in their powers. But these were refuted by the drugs written down in the first book of notes. Those drugs displayed their great powers, and they were made from simples whose composition was from contraries. And not least in the books that are to be written after this one, we shall show excellent drugs whose composition is from things contrary in their powers.

K13.563-564K. XIII 563–564, ch. 3.1, 3.2 difficulty low (0.66/100; thin 1, absent 0) (a) fail (b) pass 7/8 (c) fail 13/16 unit: fail

Greek (Kühn)

νυνὶ δὲ περὶ τῆς ἡμετέρας ἀγωγῆς, ἣν ἐπενοήσαμεν ἐπὶ τοῖς νευροτρώτοις, ὁ καθ’ ὅλον τουτὶ τὸ βιβλίον ἔσται μοι λόγος, ἐπιδεικνύντι πόσον πλῆθος φαρμάκων τὰς αὐτὰς δυνάμεις 564 ἐχόντων εὗρον, ἐκ τοῦ τόν γε καθόλου τρόπον τῆς θεραπείας ἐπινοῆσαι καὶ τὰς τῶν ἁπλῶν φαρμάκων ἐπίστασθαι δυνάμεις. [Τίς ὁ καθόλου τρόπος ἐστὶ τῆς θεραπείας τῶν νευροτρώτων καὶ τίνα τὰ κατ’ εἶδος φάρμακα.] Ἐθεασάμην μὲν ἅπαντας τοὺς διδασκάλους τῇ καλουμένῃ συνήθως ἤδη τοῖς νεωτέροις ἰατροῖς ἐναίμῳ καὶ κολλητικῇ θεραπεύοντας ἀγωγῇ. κατ’ ἀρχὰς γὰρ ἐπιτιθέντες τι φάρμακον τῶν ἐναίμων καλουμένων, ἐπειρῶντο κολλᾷν τὰ χείλη τοῦ τραύματος. εἰ δὲ φλεγμήνειεν, ὕδατί τε κατήντλουν θερμῷ δαψιλεῖ παραχέοντες ἔλαιον, ἐπίπασμά τε προσέφερον ἄλευρον πύρινον ἡψημένον ἐν ὑδρελαίῳ. καὶ μέντοι καὶ τοὺς μονομάχους, ὅσοι τε κατὰ τῆς μύλης ἐτρώθησαν ἀνωτέρω τε ταύτης κατὰ τὸν πλατὺν καὶ λεπτὸν τένοντα θεραπευομένους ὑπ’ αὐτῶν ἑώρων ὡσαύτως. ἴσως δὲ ἄμεινον ᾖ εἰπεῖν οὐ θεραπευομένους, ἀλλ’ ἀπολλυμένους, ὀλιγοστοὶ γὰρ ἐξ αὐτῶν καὶ οὗτοι χωλούμενοι διεσώζοντο.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἐπίπασμά, ᾖ

But now, throughout this whole book, my discourse will be about our own method of treatment, which we devised for those wounded in the nerves, as I show how great a multitude of drugs possessing the same powers 564 I discovered, from having conceived the general manner of the treatment and from knowing the powers of the simple drugs. [What the general manner of the treatment of those wounded in the nerves is, and what the drugs are according to kind.] I observed all my teachers treating by the method of treatment now customarily called by the more recent physicians the one "for bleeding wounds" and "agglutinative". For at the beginning, applying some one of the drugs called "for bleeding wounds", they attempted to agglutinate the lips of the wound. And if it became inflamed, they fomented it with hot water in abundance, pouring oil in alongside, and they applied as a dressing (ἐπίπασμα) wheaten flour boiled in water-and-oil. And moreover I also saw the gladiators, those who had been wounded at the kneecap and above it, at the broad and thin tendon, being treated by them in the same way. But perhaps it would be better to say not being treated, but perishing; for very few of them survived, and these became lame.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐπίπασμα, ᾖ

Now, however, my account throughout this whole book will concern our own method, which we devised for the nerve-wounded, as I show how great a number of drugs having the same powers 564 I found, from having devised the general manner of the treatment and from knowing the powers of the simple drugs. [What the general manner of treatment of the nerve-wounded is, and what the drugs are according to species.] I observed all my teachers treating by the method which is now customarily called by the more recent physicians the 'bloody' (enaimos) and agglutinative method. For at the beginning they applied one of the drugs called 'bloody' (enaima) and tried to glue together the lips of the wound. And if it became inflamed, they bathed it with hot water in abundance, pouring oil in alongside, and applied as a sprinkled application (epipasma) wheaten flour boiled in hydrelaeum (water-and-oil). Moreover, I also saw the gladiators, those who had been wounded at the kneecap and above it at the broad and thin tendon, being treated by them in the same way. But perhaps it would be better to say not 'being treated' but 'perishing'; for very few of them came through, and these were left lame.

K13.564-565K. XIII 564–565, ch. 3.2 difficulty low (0.0/100; thin 0, absent 0) (a) pass (b) pass 5/7 (c) pass 14/14 unit: pass

Greek (Kühn)

καὶ μέντοι καὶ τῶν ἐν ἄκρᾳ τῇ χειρὶ τενόντων, δι’ ὧν οἱ δάκτυλοι κάμπτονταί 565 τε καὶ ἐκτείνονται, παμπόλλους ἐθεασάμην ἤτοι θλασθέντας ἢ νυγέντας ἐλαχίστῳ νύγματι, σηπομένους οὕτως, ὡς ἐοικέναι τοῖς ἑψομένοις ἐν ὕδασι καὶ μάλιστα ἐπὶ πλέον. ὁμοίως γὰρ αὐτοῖς ἐκλύονταί τε καὶ μαλακύνονται κατὰ τὴν σύστασιν οἱ διὰ τῆς προειρημένης ἀγωγῆς θεραπευόμενοι. κατέγνων οὖν αὐτῆς, ὡς σηπούσης τὸ τετρωμένον μόριον. ἐπεὶ δὲ τὰ σηπόμενα πάντα προσεγίγνωσκον, ὑφ’ ὑγρότητός τε καὶ θερμότητος τοῦτο πάσχοντα, τὴν θεραπείαν ἔγνων χρῆναι διὰ τῶν ξηραινόντων καὶ ψυχόντων ποιεῖσθαι. πάλιν δ’ ἐννοήσας, ὡς τὸ ψυχρὸν ἀληθῶς ὑφ’ Ἱπποκράτους εἴρηται πολέμιον εἶναι τοῖς νεύροις, ἔγνων ἄριστον ἔσεσθαι φάρμακον ἐπὶ τῶν νευροτρώτων, ὅπερ ἂν ξηραίνῃ τε καὶ μέσως ἔχῃ τοῦ θερμαίνειν τε καὶ ψύχειν ἢ μὴ πολὺ τοῦ μέσου πρὸς τὸ θερμαίνειν ἀποκεχωρηκὸς εἴη. δύνασθαί γε μὴν καὶ θερμὸν ἱκανῶς εἶναι τὸ φάρμακον, ἐάν γε αὐτῷ τὸ ξηραίνειν ὑπάρχῃ. χωρὶς γὰρ ὑγρότητος οὐχ οἷόν τ’ ἐστὶ σήπειν τὸ θερμαῖνον.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

And indeed, of the tendons in the extremity of the hand, through which the fingers are bent 565 and extended, I have seen a very great many, either bruised or pricked with the slightest puncture, putrefying in such a way as to resemble things boiled in water, and especially those boiled too long. For those treated by the aforementioned method are loosened and softened in their consistency just like these. I therefore condemned it, as putrefying the wounded part. And since I already knew that all things that putrefy suffer this from moisture and heat, I recognised that the treatment must be carried out by means of drying and cooling agents. But reflecting again that cold has truly been said by Hippocrates to be hostile to the nerves, I recognised that the best drug for those wounded in the nerves would be whatever dries and is intermediate between heating and cooling, or has not departed far from the mean in the direction of heating. The drug could nevertheless even be considerably hot, provided that the drying property belongs to it. For without moisture that which heats is not able to putrefy.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: μάλιστα ἐπὶ πλέον, ἐκλύονται

And indeed, of the tendons at the extremity of the hand, through which the fingers are flexed 565 and extended, I saw very many, whether contused or pricked by the slightest puncture, putrefying in such a way as to resemble things boiled in water, and especially things boiled for a longer time. For in the same way as these, the parts treated by the aforementioned method become slack and soft in their consistency. I therefore condemned that method, as one that putrefies the wounded part. And since I further recognised that all putrefying things undergo this through moisture and heat, I judged that the treatment ought to be carried out by means of things that dry and cool. But then, reflecting that cold has truly been said by Hippocrates to be hostile to the nerves, I judged that the best drug for the nerve-wounded would be one that both dries and holds a middle position between heating and cooling, or that has not departed far from the mean in the direction of heating. Indeed, the drug can even be quite hot, provided that it has the property of drying. For without moisture it is not possible for what heats to cause putrefaction.

K13.565-566K. XIII 565–566, ch. 3.2 difficulty low (0.68/100; thin 1, absent 0) (a) pass (b) fail 4/6 (c) fail 9/12 unit: fail

Greek (Kühn)

ἔτι δὲ πρὸς τούτοις κᾀκεῖνο προσεννοήσας, ὡς πολὺ διαφέροι γυμνῷ τῷ πεπονθότι μορίῳ προσφέρειν τὸ φάρμακον ἢ διὰ σωμάτων ἑτέρων 566 προκειμένων αὐτοῦ, προσήκειν ἔγνων ἑτέροις μὲν ἐπὶ γεγυμνωμένου τοῦ νεύρου χρῆσθαι φαρμάκοις, ἑτέροις δ’ ὅταν ᾖ νενυγμένον, ἀπαθῶν τῶν προκειμένων αὐτοῦ σωμάτων ὑπαρχόντων. ἔνθα μὲν γὰρ γυμνῷ τῷ νεύρῳ προσφέροι τις τὸ φάρμακον, ἐνταῦθα τὴν εἰρημένην ἔνδειξιν τῶν ὠφελησάντων φαρμάκων εἰλικρινῆ παραλαμβάνεσθαι προσῆκεν. τῶν δὲ προκειμένων μορίων ἀπαθῶν ὄντων, λεπτομερέστερον εὑρίσκειν χρὴ φάρμακον, ὅπως ἡ δύναμις αὐτοῦ μὴ προεκλύηται διὰ τῶν ἐν μέσῳ σωμάτων, ἀλλ’ εἰς τὸ βάθος ἀφίκηται. δύνασθαι δὲ τὸ τοιοῦτο φάρμακον, ἐὰν ἰσχυρῶς πεφύκῃ ξηραίνειν, ἔτι εἰ καὶ μὴ σύμμετρον εἴη, κατὰ θερμότητα καὶ ψυχρότητα μηδὲ ἀδικεῖν, ἀσθενεστέρας αὐτοῦ γιγνομένης τῆς κατὰ τὸ θερμαίνειν ἢ ψύχειν ἐνεργείας ἐν τῷ διεξιέναι τὰ προκείμενα μόρια τοῦ νευρώδους σώματος. εὑρημένου δὲ τοῦ σκοποῦ τῶν ὠφελησάντων φαρμάκων, ἐν τῷ λεπτομερεῖ τῆς οὐσίας αὐτῶν, ἐφεξῆς ἦν ἀνα μιμνήσκεσθαι τῆς ὕλης.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἔτι εἰ καὶ

And further, in addition to these things, having also taken this into consideration, that it makes a great difference whether one applies the drug to the affected part when it is bare or through other bodies 566 lying in front of it, I recognised that it is fitting to use some drugs when the nerve has been laid bare, and others when it has been pricked while the bodies lying in front of it remain unaffected. For where one applies the drug to the bare nerve, there the aforesaid indication of the drugs that will be of benefit ought to be taken over in its pure form; but when the parts lying in front are unaffected, one must find a more fine-particled drug, so that its power is not exhausted beforehand by the bodies in between, but reaches into the depth. And such a drug, if it is by nature strongly drying, is able, even if moreover it should not be well-proportioned in respect of heat and cold, to do no harm either, since its activity in heating or cooling becomes weaker as it passes through the parts lying in front of the nervous body. Now that the aim of the drugs that will be of benefit had been found, in the fine-particled character of their substance, the next step was to recall the material.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἔτι εἰ καὶ (possibly corrupt; taken as 'even if'), ἀνα μιμνήσκεσθαι (read as ἀναμιμνήσκεσθαι), νευρώδους (nervous/sinewy, covering nerve and tendon)

Further, in addition to these, having also considered this further point, that it makes a great difference whether one applies the drug to the affected part when it is bare or through other bodies 566 lying in front of it, I recognised that it was proper to use some drugs when the nerve has been laid bare, and other drugs when it has been punctured while the bodies lying in front of it remain unaffected. For where one applies the drug to the bare nerve, there it was proper to adopt the indication already stated for the drugs that had been of benefit in its unmixed form. But when the parts lying in front are unaffected, one must find a finer-particled drug, so that its power is not dissipated beforehand in passing through the intervening bodies, but reaches the depth. And such a drug, if it is by nature strongly drying, is able to do no harm even if it should not be well-proportioned in respect of heat and cold, since its activity in heating or cooling becomes weaker while it passes through the parts lying in front of the nervous body. The aim of the drugs that had been of benefit having been found to lie in the fine-particled character of their substance, the next step was to recall the material.

Greek (Kühn)

ἔγνωστο δ’ ἐν τῇ τῶν ἁπλῶν φαρμάκων ἐπισκέψει τὰ μὲν πλεῖστα τῶν θερμαινόντων λεπτομερῆ, τὰ ﻿ 567 δὲ πλεῖστα τῶν ψυχόντων παχυμερῆ. μόνως γὰρ ἐν αὐτοῖς μία ποιότης ἡ ὀξεῖα μετὰ λεπτομεροῦς οὐσίας γίνεταί ποτε, καὶ διὰ τοῦτο πάντων τῶν ψυχόντων λεπτομερέστερόν ἐστιν ὄξος τὸ δριμύτατον, ὃ καὶ θερμότητος βραχείας μετέχειν ἀποδέδεικται. τῶν δ’ αὖ θερμαινόντων ὀποὶ καλοὶ λεπτομερέστατοι τυγχάνουσιν ὄντες καὶ πᾶσιν ἀνθρώποις ἤδη γινώσκεται πρωτεύων αὐτῶν ὁ Κυρηναῖος, ἀλλὰ τούτου μὴ παρόντος καὶ τῷ Μηδικῷ χρώμεθα καὶ τοῖς μετ’ αὐτῶν ἀπὸ τῶν ἀνατολικῶν χωρίων κομιζομένοις, ὁποῖός ἐστι καὶ ὁ σαγαπηνὸς ὀνομαζόμενος. ἐκ ταὐτοῦ δὲ γένους ὑπάρχει τοῖσδε καὶ ὁ τοῦ εὐφορβίου κατὰ τὰ δυτικὰ μέρη τῆς οἰκουμένης γεννώμενος, εἴτ’ ὀπὸν αὐτὸν εἴτ’ ἄλλο τι καλεῖν ἐθέλοι τις. ἡμῖν δ’ ἀρκέσει καθ’ ὅλον τὸν λόγον εὐφόρβιον ὀνομάζειν τὸ φάρμακον τοῦτο, καθάπερ ἔθος ἤδη πᾶσίν ἐστιν. ἀλλὰ καὶ τῶν κατὰ τὴν ἡμετέραν χώραν γεννωμένων ὅ τε τοῦ πάνακος ὀπὸς καὶ οἱ τῶν τιθυμάλλων λεπτομεροῦς εἰσι δυνάμεως. καὶ μέντοι καὶ θερμαίνουσιν οἱ εἰρημένοι πάντες ὀποὶ γενναίως.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: λεπτομερέστερόν (superlative sense taken, though form is comparative)

And it had been established in the investigation of simple drugs that most of the heating ones are fine-particled, 567 while most of the cooling ones are thick-particled. For among them only one quality, the sour, sometimes occurs together with a fine-particled substance, and for this reason the sharpest vinegar is the most fine-particled of all cooling things, which has also been demonstrated to share in a slight heat. Of the heating ones, in turn, good juices (opoi) happen to be the most fine-particled, and it is already known to all men that the Cyrenaic one holds first place among them; but when this is not available we use also the Median one and those brought along with them from the eastern regions, such as the one called sagapenum. Of the same kind as these is also that of euphorbium, which is produced in the western parts of the inhabited world, whether one wishes to call it a juice or something else. For us it will suffice throughout the whole discourse to name this drug euphorbium, as is already the custom for everyone. But also, among those produced in our own country, both the juice of panax and those of the spurges are of fine-particled power. And indeed all the aforementioned juices heat vigorously.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: μόνως, μία ποιότης ἡ ὀξεῖα

It had been established in the investigation of simple drugs that most of the heating drugs are fine-particled, and 567 most of the cooling ones thick-particled. For among these [the cooling ones] only one quality, the sour, is sometimes found together with a fine-particled substance; and for this reason the sharpest vinegar is finer-particled than all the cooling things, and it has also been shown to partake of a slight heat. Among the heating drugs, in turn, the saps are the finest-particled, and the foremost of them, as all men already know, is the Cyrenaic; but when this is not to hand we also use the Median, and those brought along with them from the eastern regions, such as the one called sagapenum. Of the same kind as these is also the sap of euphorbium produced in the western parts of the inhabited world, whether one wishes to call it a sap or something else. For our part it will suffice throughout the whole discussion to call this drug euphorbium, as is now the custom with everyone. But also among those produced in our own country, the sap of panax and the saps of the spurges are of fine-particled power. And indeed all the saps mentioned heat vigorously.

Greek (Kühn)

ὁ δὲ τοῦ ὀποβαλσάμου λεπτομερὴς 568 ὤν καὶ αὐτὸς οὐ πάνυ θερμαίνει, λεπτομερῆ δ’ οὐσίαν ἔχει. καὶ τῶν γεωδῶν ὅ τ’ ἀφρὸς τοῦ νίτρου καὶ τὸ ῥᾳδίως ὕδατι λυόμενον ἀφρόνιτρον, ὡς ἐν γεώδεσι σώμασι δηλονότι ἀκουόντων ὑμῶν. ἐφεξῆς δ’ αὐτῶν ἐστι τὸ Βερενίκιον ὀνομαζόμενον νίτρον ἥ τ’ Ἀσία πέτρα. τὸ δ’ ἄνθος αὐτῆς ἁπάντων ἐστὶ τῶν γεωδῶν λεπτομερέστατον. ἐκ δὲ τῶν μεταλλευομένων ἀρσενικόν τε καὶ θεῖόν ἐστι λεπτομερῆ τε καὶ θερμά. τούτων δ’ ἐφεξῆς τὸ μίσυ καὶ ἡ τοῦ χαλκοῦ λεπὶς καὶ χαλκῖτις ἔχοντά τι καὶ στύψεως, διὸ καὶ καυθείσῃ τῇ χαλκίτιδι χρώμεθα τὰ πολλὰ πρὸς τὰς τῶν λεπτομερεστέρων φαρμάκων συνθέσεις. ἄνευ δὲ τοῦ θερμαίνειν ἐπιφανῶς λεπτομερῆ μεταλλικὰ φάρμακα, σπόδιόν τε καὶ πομφόλυξ ἐστὶ καὶ τὸ καλούμενον ψωρικόν. λεπτομερὴς δὲ ἱκανῶς ἐστι καὶ ἡ χρυσοκόλλη. ἐν δὲ τοῖς χυλοῖς ἐμάθομεν εἶναι λεπτομερῆ δάφνινον μὲν ἅμα τῷ θερμαίνειν ἰσχυρῶς, ὡς πρὸς τὴν αἴσθησιν. οὐδὲν γὰρ ἧττον αὐτοῦ θερμαῖνον τῇ δυνάμει τὸ κέδρινον ἀπολείπεται τῇ κατὰ τὴν αἴσθησιν θερμότητι διὰ τὴν λεπτομέρειαν.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ὁ δὲ τοῦ ὀποβαλσάμου, χυλοῖς, δάφνινον, κέδρινον

But the juice of opobalsamum, being fine-particled 568 itself too, does not heat very much, yet has a fine-particled substance. And among the earthy substances, the froth of nitron and the aphronitron which is easily dissolved in water [are fine-particled], you understanding this, of course, as among earthy bodies. Next after these are the nitron called Berenician and the Asian stone; and the flower of the latter is the finest-particled of all the earthy substances. Among the mined substances, arsenic and sulphur are both fine-particled and hot. Next after these are misy and scale of copper and chalcitis, which also have something of astringency; for which reason we use chalcitis in its burnt state for the most part for the compositions of the finer-particled drugs. Fine-particled metallic drugs without manifest heating are spodion and pompholyx and what is called psorikon. Chrysocolla too is fairly fine-particled. Among the juices we learned that the laurel [oil] is fine-particled together with heating strongly, as regards sensation; for the cedar [oil], which heats no less than it in its potency, falls short in the heat perceived by sensation because of its fineness of parts.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: Ἀσία πέτρα, ψωρικόν, χυλοῖς, δάφνινον, ὡς ἐν γεώδεσι σώμασι δηλονότι ἀκουόντων ὑμῶν

The [juice] of opobalsam, although it too is fine-particled, does not heat very much; but it has a fine-particled substance. Among earthy things, the foam of nitre and the aphronitre that is readily dissolved by water [are fine-particled], you understanding of course [that they are so] as among earthy bodies. Next after them comes the nitre called Berenician, and the Asian stone. The flower of this [stone] is the most fine-particled of all earthy things. Among mined substances, arsenikon and sulphur are both fine-particled and hot. Next after these come misy, copper flake and chalcitis, which also have something of astringency; for this reason we mostly use chalcitis burnt for the compositions of the more fine-particled drugs. Mineral drugs that are fine-particled without heating perceptibly are spodium, pompholyx and the so-called psoricum. Chrysocolla too is quite fine-particled. Among the juices we have learnt that laurel [oil] is fine-particled and at the same time heats strongly, so far as perception is concerned. For cedar [oil], although it heats no less than it in potency, falls short in heat as perceived because of its fineness of parts.

Greek (Kühn)

ἐκ τούτου δὲ γένους ἐστὶ τῷ 569 κεδρίνῳ καὶ ἡ στακτὴ καλουμένη κονία. καὶ ταύτης αὐτῆς, ἥτις ἂν ἐξ ὕλης λεπτομεροῦς γένηται, καυθείσης, ὑπερβάλλει τῇ δυνάμει τὸ κέδρινον ἔλαιον. ἐκ μηλέας τε οὖν καὶ συκῆς καὶ μᾶλλόν γε τῆς ἀγρίας, ἣν ἐρινεὸν ὀνομάζουσιν, ἔκ τε τῶν τιθυμάλλων καιομένων τὴν τοιαύτην ἐργαζόμεθα κονίαν. ἄριστος δὲ τῶν τιθυμάλλων ὁ χαρακίας προσαγορευόμενος. ἐκ δὲ τῶν σιτηρῶν ὀνομαζομένων σπερμάτων λεπτομερέστατος μὲν ὁ ὄροβος, ἄνευ τοῦ θερμαίνειν ἐπιφανῶς. ἡ δ’ αἶρα μετὰ τοῦ θερμαίνειν σαφῶς. ἡ κριθὴ δ’ οὐδ’ ὅλως θερμαίνει. μέσης δ’ ὕλης ἐν αὐτοῖς ἐστι τὰ πικρὰ ταῖς ποιότησι, τῶν μὲν πάνυ λεπτομερῶν ἀπολειπόμενα, παχυμερὲς δ’ οὐδὲν ἔχοντα. τινὰ δ’ αὐτῶν ἀποκεχώρηκε βραχὺ τῶν μέσων, τὰ μὲν ὡς ἐπὶ τὸ θερμαίνειν, τὰ δὲ ὡς ἐπὶ τὸ ψύχειν, ὧν ἁπάντων τὰς ὕλας ἐν τῇ περὶ τῶν ἁπλῶν φαρμάκων πραγματείᾳ διῆλθον, ὥσπερ γε καὶ τῶν ἀκροδρύων, ἐν οἷς ἐστι τὰ ἀμύγδαλα τὰ πικρὰ καὶ τὰ πιστάκια.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: στακτὴ, χαρακίας

Of this same kind as cedar oil 569 is also the so-called strained lye. And of this very lye, whichever is produced from a fine-particled material that has been burnt surpasses cedar oil in power. So it is from the apple tree and the fig tree, and still more the wild fig, which they call erineos, and from the spurges, when burnt, that we prepare such a lye. The best of the spurges is the one called charakias. Among the seeds called cereal seeds, the most fine-particled is bitter vetch, without heating noticeably; darnel, with clearly heating; barley does not heat at all. Of intermediate material among them are those that are bitter in their qualities, falling short of the very fine-particled ones, but having nothing thick-particled. Some of them have departed slightly from the intermediate, some in the direction of heating, others in the direction of cooling; the materials of all of these I went through in the treatise on simple drugs, just as also those of the tree-fruits, among which are the bitter almonds and the pistachios.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: στακτὴ, ἐκ τούτου δὲ γένους ἐστὶ τῷ κεδρίνῳ, αἶρα, ἀκροδρύων

Of this same kind as cedar oil 569 is also the so-called percolated lye. And of this lye itself, the one that is made from a fine-particled material when it has been burnt surpasses cedar oil in potency. We prepare such lye, then, from the apple tree and the fig, and still more from the wild fig, which they call caprifig (erineos), and from spurges when they are burnt. The best of the spurges is the one called characias. Among the seeds called cereal seeds, bitter vetch is the most fine-particled, without heating perceptibly; darnel (aira) is fine-particled while clearly heating; barley does not heat at all. Of intermediate substance among them are those that are bitter in their qualities: they fall short of the very fine-particled ones, but have nothing thick-particled. Some of them have departed a little from the intermediate ones, some in the direction of heating, others in the direction of cooling. I went through the materials of all these in the treatise on simple drugs, as I did also for the tree-fruits, among which are bitter almonds and pistachios.

Greek (Kühn)

τῆς δὲ ἀπὸ τῶν φυτῶν ὕλης οὐ μέμνημαι κατὰ τὸ παρὸν, ἀνελθεῖν γὰρ ἐπὶ τὴν ἀρχὴν βούλομαι καὶ διηγήσασθαι, πότε καὶ 570 πῶς ἐχρησάμην ἑκάστῳ τῶν ἐπινοηθέντων. ἐκ τούτου γὰρ οἶμαι τοὺς εὐφυεῖς τῶν νέων προτραπήσεσθαι, γνῶναι τὴν ὕλην τῶν φαρμάκων αὐτόπτας αὐτῆς γενομένους οὐχ ἅπαξ ἤ δὶς, ἀλλὰ πολλάκις. ἡ γὰρ τῶν αἰσθητῶν διάγνωσις ἐκ συνεχοῦς θέας ἀκριβοῦται, καὶ τούτου μέγιστόν ἐστι τεκμήριον ἡ τῶν ἀπαραλλάκτων μὲν ἡμῖν εἶναι δοκούντων διδύμων, οὐκ ἀπαραλλάκτων δὲ τοῖς συνήθεσι μὴ παραδραμεῖν. τὴν μὲν οὖν ἀπό τε βοτανῶν καὶ θάμνων καὶ δένδρων ὕλην ἄριστόν ἐστι πρὶν ἐξαιρεθῆναι τῆς γῆς σκοπεῖσθαι κατὰ τὰς ἐγκαρπώσεις, μελλούσας τε καὶ ἀρχομένας καὶ προϊούσας καὶ ἀκμαζούσας. αὕτη γὰρ ἡ συνεχὴς αὐτῶν θέα διδάξει σε πηνίκα μάλιστα καλῶς ἄν τις αὐτὰ λαμβάνων καὶ ἐν οἴκοις ξηροῖς τιθέμενος φυλάττοι, μήθ’ ὑπ’ ἀκτίνων ἡλιακῶν ἐξοπτώμενα μήτε ὑπὸ νοτίδος ἐκ τῆς ὀροφῆς ἢ τοίχων ὑγραινόμενα. γένοιτο δ’ ἂν τοῦτο τῶν οἴκων μήτε καταγείων ὄντων μήτε προσχόρων, μήθ’ ὑπὸ κεράμων πρὸς μεσημβρίαν μέντοι τὰς θυρίδας ἐστραμμένας ἐχόντων. ἀλλὰ τά γε φάρμακα μὴ πλησίον αὐτῶν ἀποκείσθω.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: παραδραμεῖν, προσχόρων, ἐγκαρπώσεις

Of the material derived from plants I make no mention at present, for I wish to go back to the beginning and to recount when and 570 how I made use of each of the things that were devised. For from this I think the naturally gifted among the young will be encouraged to come to know the material of drugs by becoming eyewitnesses of it themselves, not once or twice, but many times. For the recognition of perceptible things is perfected through continuous viewing, and the greatest proof of this is the case of twins, who seem to us to be indistinguishable but are not indistinguishable to those familiar with them, so that they do not pass unnoticed (παραδραμεῖν). Now the material from herbs, shrubs and trees it is best to examine, before it is taken up out of the earth, at the stages of fruiting—impending, beginning, advancing and at full maturity. For this continuous viewing of them will teach you at what season above all one would do well to gather them and, placing them in dry rooms, to preserve them, neither baked by the rays of the sun nor moistened by damp from the roof or the walls. This would come about if the rooms are neither underground nor προσχόρων (adjoining the ground?), nor under tiles, having, however, their windows turned toward the south. But let the drugs at any rate not be stored near these.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: μὴ παραδραμεῖν, προσχόρων, μήθ’ ὑπὸ κεράμων πρὸς μεσημβρίαν μέντοι, αὐτῶν (in μὴ πλησίον αὐτῶν), ἐγκαρπώσεις

Of the material derived from plants I make no mention for the present, for I wish to go back to the beginning and relate when and 570 how I made use of each of the things I devised. For from this, I think, the naturally gifted among the young will be urged on to come to know the material of drugs by becoming eyewitnesses of it, not once or twice, but many times. For the discrimination of perceptible things is made exact by continual observation, and the greatest proof of this is the case of twins who seem to us indistinguishable, but who are not indistinguishable to those familiar with them, so that these do not mistake them (μὴ παραδραμεῖν). Now the material from herbs, shrubs and trees it is best to examine before it is taken out of the earth, through the stages of its fruiting: when the fruiting is about to begin, when it is beginning, when it is advancing, and when it is at its peak. For this continual observation of them will teach you at what time one would do best to take them and to keep them, placing them in dry rooms, so that they are neither baked by the rays of the sun nor moistened by damp from the ceiling or the walls. This would come about if the rooms are neither underground nor at ground level (?), nor under roof-tiles, while having their windows turned toward the south. But the drugs at any rate should not be stored near them.

And I urge my companions 571 to imitate me in this respect too, at least if they wish to perform well the works of the art. For you know how the finest drugs are conveyed to me from each of the nations every year, because the wretched sellers of petty wares (ῥωποπῶλαι) corrupt them in every possible way. But it is perhaps better to blame not these alone, but much more than them the merchants who transport them, and still more than those the men who are called root-cutters, but who bring down from the mountains to the cities no less than roots also juices and saps and fruits, flowers and shoots; for these are the first of all to practise villainy upon them. Whoever, then, wishes to have an abundance of remedies from every source, let him become experienced in all the material from plants, and in all that from animals and from metals, and in all the earthy bodies which we bring into medical use without mining, so as to distinguish among them the genuine and the spurious; and after this let him exercise himself in my treatise in which I wrote about the power of simple drugs.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

For if he does not come thus prepared to the 572 benefit to be had from these commentaries, he will know the method only as far as discourse goes, but will accomplish no work worthy of it. For suppose that someone knows the very things I have said above about those wounded in the nerves, but through ignorance puts adulterated drugs into the compounds he makes, or does not even know their powers accurately in the first place: will it not be necessary that this man fail more often than he succeeds? To me, at any rate, it certainly seems so. Now, knowing the powers accurately differs very greatly from merely knowing them. For merely knowing is to recognize whether the drug is of such a nature as to dry or moisten or cool or heat us; but knowing accurately is, in addition to this, also to have come to know the quantity of the power. For some drugs have a power generative of lukewarm heat when they come into contact with our bodies, some of a moderate heat, just as others are a little stronger than this, and others still of a heat already so strongly boiling that it is able to burn. Accordingly the physician must aim not only at the quality of the condition, but also at what one might call the quantity in it.

573 For 'quantity' in the case of quality is not said in an altogether proper sense. It is said nonetheless, just as a fever too is called great and small. And so extensive is the use of things said in this way that they already possess the force of a proper term, much as with 'box' (pyxis), 'coppersmith' (chalkeus), 'painter' (zōgraphos) and 'woodcutter' (dryotomos), and, to put it in a word, with those terms that began from what the grammarians call catachresis but later came to be accepted as being said in the proper sense. These things have been said by me on account of those who engage in dialectic at the wrong moment. What I left off saying is this: that what needs to be dried does not need simply that which dries, but with the appropriate measure; what has advanced into much moisture needs that which dries very much, what has advanced into little needs that which does this slightly, just as what has advanced into much needs that which dries greatly, though not extremely nor to the utmost. For the quantity of the dryness must always be proportionate to the quantity of the moisture.

I myself, then, when I first ventured to alter the common course of the treatment of those wounded in the nerves, aimed at estimating how great a degree of moisture and heat the 574 affected body had come to. And when a certain young man was shown to me who, at the first joint of the middle finger of the right hand, as a result of a contusion had the whole region around the nerve at that place so moist that it was already putrefying, the putrefying part itself I plastered with a poultice of dripped lye and raw lysis—know that it is barley meal that I call by this name—while the part that was not putrefying but was tense and painful I fomented by pouring over it plenty of warm oil, and then, having wiped it off with dry wool, I smeared around it a cerate as moist as we use in the case of fractures, having euphorbium mixed into it. This too had been prepared by me according to my own devising on an occasion of the following kind. Someone came to me in a severe winter and, showing his knee, said that it readily suffered when it had become chilled, but was immediately benefited by heating agents; and he asked to receive some such drug, for having travelled on foot in the cold he had been chilled and was in more violent pain than at other times. "Now then," he said, "I am going off to bathe, and I shall send for the drug from the bath."

'Now then,' he said, 'I am going off to bathe, and I shall send for the drug from the bath-house.'

ὁ μὲν ταῦτ’ εἰπὼν ᾤχετο· 575 μηδὲν δ’ ἔχων ἕτοιμον τοιοῦτο φάρμακον, εὐφόρβιον ἐλείωσα μετ’ ἐλαίου, καὶ τήξας κηρὸν ἔμιξα καὶ μικρὸν ὕστερον, ἡνίκα ἔπεμψεν ὁ φίλος αἰτῶν τὸ φάρμακον, ἔδωκα. τάχιστα δ’ ἀνωδύνου γενομένου προὐτράπην ἐντεῦθεν ἐπ’ ἄλλων μορίων ὁμοίως πεπονθότων χρήσασθαι, περὶ ὧν αὖθις εἰρήσεται. νυνὶ δ’ ἐπὶ τὸν τοῦ νευροτρώτου λόγον ἐπάνειμι διὰ τοὺς μηδὲν μὲν τούτων ὧν ἐγὼ νῦν ὑφηγοῦμαι γινώσκοντας, ἐγκαλοῦντας δὲ λεξειδίοις· οὓς εὑρεῖν μαντεύομαι κακῶς ὀνομάζειν νευροτρώτους, ὅσοι τῶν τενόντων ἐτρώθησαν. τένοντας γὰρ αὐτοὺς οἶμαι καλεῖν τοὺς ἔξωθεν τῆς χειρὸς ἐπιτεταμένους, ὅλῳ τε τῷ καρπῷ καὶ τοῖς δακτύλοις. ἐγὼ δ’ ὅτι μὲν σαφέστερον καὶ κυριώτερον ἦν αὐτοὺς ὀνομάζεσθαι τενοντοτρώτους οὐκ ἀγνοῶ· πάντων δ’ ἀνδρῶν συνήθως καλούντων νευροτρώτους, ὅπως μή τίς με νεωτεροποιεῖν ἐν ταῖς προσηγορίαις φῇ, τὴν συνήθη προειλόμην ἀντὶ τῆς ἀληθοῦς. εἶχε δὲ οὐδ’ αὐτὴ συνήθης γεγονέναι, δίχα λόγου φθασάντων τῶν πλείστων ἰατρῶν καὶ τοὺς τένοντας ὀνομάζειν νεῦρα.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: εὑρεῖν μαντεύομαι, εἶχε δὲ οὐδ’ αὐτὴ συνήθης γεγονέναι

Having said this, he went away; 575 and since I had no such drug ready, I ground euphorbium smooth with oil, and having melted wax I mixed it in, and a little later, when my friend sent asking for the drug, I gave it. And since he very quickly became free of pain, I was prompted from then on to use it on other parts similarly affected, about which I shall speak again. But now I return to the account of the one wounded in the nerves, on account of those who know nothing of these things which I am now expounding, but find fault over petty words; who, I prophesy, will find that I wrongly name as 'wounded in the nerves' all those who have been wounded in the tendons. For I suppose they call 'tendons' those stretched over the outside of the hand, both over the whole wrist and over the fingers. I for my part am not unaware that it would be clearer and more proper for them to be named 'wounded in the tendons'; but since all men customarily call them 'wounded in the nerves', in order that no one may say that I am innovating in the appellations, I preferred the customary term to the true one. And not even this term could have become customary, had not most physicians already, without reason, come to call the tendons too 'nerves'.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: οὓς εὑρεῖν μαντεύομαι, λεξειδίοις, εἶχε δὲ οὐδ’ αὐτὴ συνήθης γεγονέναι, δίχα λόγου

Having said this, he went away. 575 Having no such drug ready, I ground euphorbium fine with oil, and having melted wax I mixed it in; and a little later, when my friend sent asking for the drug, I gave it. When he very quickly became free of pain, I was prompted by this to use it on other parts similarly affected, about which I shall speak again later. But now I return to the account of the nerve-wounded, because of those who know nothing of the things I am now expounding, yet find fault with petty words. These, I divine, will find that it is wrong to call 'nerve-wounded' those who have been wounded in the tendons. For I think they call 'tendons' the structures stretched along the outer side of the hand, over the whole wrist and the fingers. For my part, I am not unaware that it would be clearer and more proper for them to be named 'tendon-wounded'. But since all men customarily call them 'nerve-wounded', I preferred the customary name to the true one, so that no one might say that I am innovating in names. Yet not even this name could itself have become customary had not most physicians already, without reason, taken to calling the tendons 'nerves' as well.

ἀλλ’ εἴτε νευροτρώτους ἐθέλοις 576 ὀνομάζειν αὐτοὺς, εἴτε τενοντοτρώτους, οὔτε σωθήσονται διὰ τὰς φωνὰς ταύτας οὔτε σπασθήσονται, καθάπερ οὐδὲ κυλλωθήσονται καὶ τεθνήξονται. προσηγορία γὰρ οὔτε ἔσωσέ ποτε ἄνθρωπον οὔτ’ ἀπέκτεινεν, ἀλλ’ ἡ τῶν φαρμάκων δύναμις ἑκάτερον τούτων ἐργάζεται. κᾀγὼ ταῦθ’ ὑπαλλάττων τοὺς σηπομένους καὶ σπωμένους καὶ κεκυλλωμένους, ἐνίους δὲ καὶ διαφθειρομένους, ὑγιεῖς καὶ ζῶντας ἐργάζομαι. καὶ γὰρ αὖ καὶ τοὺς τεθλασμένους τὰ νεῦρα, καθάπερ ἐκεῖνος ἐτέθλαστο, νευροτρώτους ἐφθάκασι καλεῖν, οὐ νευροθλάστους. ἡ δὲ θεραπεία κοινὴ καὶ ὁ κίνδυνος ἴσος ἀμφοῖν ἐστι τῶν τεθλασμένων τι καὶ τρωθέντων ἤτοι νεῦρα ἢ τένοντας.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

But whether you wish to call them 576 those wounded in the nerves or those wounded in the tendons, they will neither be saved on account of these words nor be convulsed, just as they will neither be maimed nor die. For an appellation has never saved a man nor killed one; rather, it is the power of the drugs that brings about each of these. And I, by changing these drugs, make those who are putrefying and being convulsed and maimed, and some of them even perishing, healthy and alive. For again, those who have had their nerves contused, as that man had been contused, they have already come to call those wounded in the nerves, not those contused in the nerves. But the treatment is common and the danger equal for both, those who have had something contused and those who have been wounded, whether in nerves or in tendons.

But whether you wish 576 to call them 'nerve-wounded' or 'tendon-wounded', they will neither be saved by these words nor be seized by spasm because of them, just as they will not be crippled or die because of them either. For a name has never yet saved a man, nor killed one; it is the power of the drugs that brings about each of these outcomes. And I, by changing these, make those who are putrefying and in spasm and crippled, and some who are even perishing, healthy and alive. For moreover physicians have long been accustomed to call those whose nerves have been crushed, as that man's had been crushed, 'nerve-wounded', not 'nerve-crushed'. But the treatment is common to both, and the danger equal, those who have had some part crushed and those who have been wounded, whether in nerves or in tendons.

At any rate, that man, who was already putrefying in the parts around the first joint of the middle finger, and was suffering tension and pain in the parts lying above, became much better than he had been on the following day, on which, in place of the raw lysis (ὠμὴ λύσις), I mixed the flour of bitter vetch with dripped lye; and on the third day, while still being treated in the same way, he was already completely free of pain in the other parts, but at the contusion a certain sinewy body 577 resembling a thick membrane protruded, which, when I moved it moderately, easily fell out whole, being half-rotten. It seemed, then, to all those who saw it that this body was the whole nerve, rotted through, since they did not know how these tendons, clothed in sinewy membranes, both pass forward through the wrist and are stretched along the whole length of the fingers. But to me, when I had also drawn apart the rotted part of the contusion, the tendon appeared to be preserved. So, dissolving one of the trochisci, or cyclisci, or however one may wish to name them, of which one is also that of Polyeides, in boiled-down must (hepsema) or siraion—for this too you may be permitted to call as you yourself wish—and having first anointed the visible part of the nerve, I again used in the same way both the cataplasm and the unguent of euphorbium upon the same places, as before with each. And on the fourth day, seeing that everything around the first joint of the finger—previously contused, but at that time ulcerated—had subsided, I considered to what I should pass over from the cataplasm.

That man, at any rate, who was already putrefying in the region of the first joint of the middle finger, and was suffering tension and pain in the parts lying above, became much better than he had been on the following day, on which, instead of the raw solution, I mixed the meal of bitter vetch with strained lye; and on the third day, while still being treated in the same way, he was by now entirely free of pain in other respects, but at the contusion a nerve-like body 577 resembling a thick membrane protruded, which, when I moved it moderately, came away easily and whole, being half-putrefied. ἐνενόησα τοίνυν, εἰ μὲν ἀκριβῶς ἀφλέγμαντον εἴη 578 τὸ νεῦρον, ἐπὶ τὰ συνουλοῦντα μεταβαίνειν· εἰ δέ τι τῆς φλεγμονῆς αὐτοῦ σκιῤῥῶδες ὑπολείποιτο, τῶν ἱκανῶς τι ξηραινόντων προσφέρειν. ἐπεὶ τοίνυν ἐφαίνετο λείπεσθαί τι τῷ διὰ τῆς ῥίζης τοῦ δρακοντίου φαρμάκῳ πρότερον ἐχρησάμην, ὃ τὰ μὲν ἄλλα ταὐτὰ ἔχει τῷ Αἰγυπτίῳ φαρμάκῳ, τῇ ἴσιδι καλουμένῃ, προσείληφε δὲ τὴν τοῦ δρακοντίου ῥίζαν. ἐφεξῆς δὲ τούτου τῇ Ἀθηνᾷ καλουμένῃ χρησάμενος ἀπ’ ἐκείνης λοιπὸν ἐπὶ τὰ συνουλοῦντα παρεγενόμην. ἐφ’ ἑτέρου δὲ τῷ διὰ τοῦ σιραίου λελυμένῳ τροχίσκῳ καταχρίων τὰ γεγυμνωμένα μέρη τοῦ τραύματος, ἔξωθεν ἐπέβαλλον τὸ εὐφόρβιον, ἀναμίξας κηρῷ καὶ ῥητίνῃ τοσούτοις τὸ πλῆθος ὡς ἐμπλαστρῶδές τε ἅμα γενέσθαι καὶ διασώζειν τὴν δύναμιν τοῦ εὐφορβίου. πῶς δ’ ἄν τις ἀποβλέπων πρὸς τούτους τοὺς σκοποὺς συνθείη τὸ φάρμακον, ἐφεξῆς ἐρῶ. καὶ γάρ τοι καὶ διὰ τοῦ ὄξους ἄλλο φάρμακον ἐποίησα καὶ κατ’ ἐκεῖνο σκοπὸν ἔχων τήν τε σύστασιν αὐτοῦ καὶ τὴν τοῦ ὄξους λεπτομερῆ δύναμιν, ὅπως φυλάττοιτο μετὰ τοῦ τὴν ψυκτικὴν δύναμιν ἐκλύεσθαι.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἐκλύεσθαι

I considered, accordingly, that if the nerve were completely free of inflammation, 578 I should pass on to the cicatrizing drugs; but if some scirrhous remnant of its inflammation were left behind, I should apply one of the drugs that dry sufficiently. Since, then, something appeared to be left, I first used the drug made with the root of dracontium, which in other respects is the same as the Egyptian drug called Isis, but has taken in addition the root of dracontium. After this, having used the drug called Athena, from that I then came at last to the cicatrizing drugs. In another case, smearing the bared parts of the wound with the troche dissolved in siraion, I applied euphorbium on the outside, having mixed it with wax and resin in such a quantity that it became plaster-like and at the same time preserved the power of the euphorbium. How one might compound the drug with these aims in view, I shall say next. For indeed I also made another drug with vinegar, having as my aim in that case too its consistency and the fine-particled power of the vinegar, so that this might be preserved while the cooling power was dissipated.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: λείπεσθαί τι, ἐκλύεσθαι

I therefore resolved that, if the nerve were entirely free of inflammation, 578 I would pass on to the cicatrizing remedies; but if some scirrhous remnant of its inflammation were left behind, I would apply one of the strongly drying remedies. Since, then, something did appear to remain, I first used the drug made with the root of dracontium, which has all its other ingredients the same as the Egyptian drug called Isis but has the root of dracontium added. After this I used the one called Athena, and from that I then went on to the cicatrizing remedies. In another case, smearing the exposed parts of the wound with the pastille dissolved in siraeum (boiled-down must), I laid euphorbium over it on the outside, having mixed it with wax and resin in such quantity that it would at once become plaster-like and preserve the potency of the euphorbium. How one might compound the drug while looking to these aims I shall state next. For I also made another drug with vinegar, and in that one too I had as my aim both its consistency and that the fine-particled potency of the vinegar should be preserved while its cooling potency was dissipated.

περὶ μέντοι τούτων ὅπως συνετέθη, 579 μετ’ ὀλίγον εἰρήσεται, νυνὶ δ’ ἐπειδὴ τὴν ἀγωγὴν τῆς τῶν νευροτρώτων θεραπείας ἐπενόησα διὰ λογικῆς ἐνδείξεως, ἄνευ τῆς ληρώδους περιπτώσεως τῶν ἐμπειρικῶν ὅλην εἰπεῖν αὐτὴν βούλομαι, μηδένα παραλείπων διορισμὸν, ἀπ’ ἀρχῆς αὖθις ἀναλαβὼν τὸν λόγον. ὅταν γάρ τινα τετρωμένον εὐθέως παραλάβω, ποτὲ μὲν διὰ τῶν ὑγρῶν φαρμάκων ποιοῦμαι τὴν θεραπείαν, ἔστι δ’ ὅτε διὰ τῶν ἐμπλάστρων, ἐπιβαλὼν ἔξωθεν ἔριον ὡς μαλακώτατον, ἐλαίῳ θερμῷ βεβρεγμένον. εἰ δὲ τῷ δι’ ὄξους χρώμενος εἴην φαρμάκῳ, μιγνὺς ὄξος ὀλίγον τῷ ἐλαίῳ. καὶ λύω γε δὶς ἢ τρὶς ἡμέρας καὶ καταντλῶ δι’ ἐλαίου θερμοῦ. μεγάλων δ’ οὐσῶν τῶν νυκτῶν καὶ κατὰ ταύτας εἴωθα λύειν. καὶ μᾶλλόν γε ποιῶ τοῦτο δακνομένου τοῦ ἕλκους ὑπὸ τῶν φαρμάκων, ὡς ὅταν γε ἀπ’ ἀρχῆς χρωμένῳ μοι τοιούτῳ φαρμάκῳ, μήτε δάκνεσθαι λέγῃ μήτε ὀδυνᾶσθαι μήτε τάσεως ὁ κάμνων αἴσθηται, δὶς τῆς ἡμέρας ἐπιλύω τε καὶ καταντλῶ, περὶ μὲν τὴν ἕω τὸ πρῶτον, εἰς ἑσπέραν δὲ τὸ δεύτερον. ἔστω δὲ καταντλώμενον ἔλαιον ἱκανῶς μὲν θερμὸν, ἀλλ’ ἄχρι τοῦ μὴ λυπεῖν τὸ τετρωμένον.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ὡς ὅταν γε

Concerning these, however, how they were compounded 579 will be stated a little later; but now, since I devised the course of treatment of those wounded in the nerves through rational indication, without the silly chance encounter of the Empiricists, I wish to state it in its entirety, omitting no distinction, taking up the account again from the beginning. For whenever I take over someone who has just been wounded, sometimes I carry out the treatment by means of the liquid drugs, and sometimes by means of the plasters, applying on the outside wool as soft as possible, soaked in warm oil. But if I happen to be using the drug made with vinegar, I mix a little vinegar with the oil. And I undo the dressing twice or three times a day and foment with warm oil. And when the nights are long, I am accustomed to undo it during these as well. And I do this the more when the wound is being bitten by the drugs, inasmuch as whenever, when I am using such a drug from the beginning, the patient says he is neither bitten nor in pain, nor perceives any tension, I undo the dressing and foment twice a day, the first time around dawn, the second toward evening. Let the oil that is poured on be sufficiently warm, but only up to the point of not distressing the wounded part.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὡς ὅταν γε, καταντλῶ, ἀγωγὴν

How these were compounded, however, 579 will be stated a little later. For now, since I devised the course of treatment of the 'nerve-wounded' by rational indication, without the nonsensical accidental encounter (periptōsis) of the Empiricists, I wish to set it out in its entirety, omitting no distinction and taking up the account again from the beginning. Whenever I take on someone who has just been wounded, I sometimes carry out the treatment with the liquid drugs and sometimes with the plasters, laying over them on the outside wool as soft as possible, soaked in warm oil. If I am using the drug made with vinegar, I mix a little vinegar into the oil. I unbind the dressing two or three times a day and pour warm oil over the part. When the nights are long, I am accustomed to unbind it during the night as well. I do this all the more when the wound is being bitten by the drugs. For when I have used such a drug from the beginning and the patient says he feels neither biting nor pain, and perceives no tension, I unbind and pour oil over the part twice a day, the first time around dawn and the second towards evening. Let the oil that is poured over be quite hot, but only so far as not to distress the wounded part.

Greek (Kühn)

ὥσπερ γὰρ τὸ ψυχρὸν ἐναντιώτατόν 580 ἐστι ταῖς τοιαύταις διαθέσεσιν, οὕτω τὸ χλιαρὸν οὐ τελέως ὠφέλιμον. ἐμάθομεν γὰρ ἐν τῷ πρώτῳ τῆς τῶν ἁπλῶν φαρμάκων δυνάμεως, ὥσπερ τὸ ὕδωρ ἐναντιώτατα πέφυκε ποιεῖν ἐν τῷ θερμὸν ἢ ψυχρὸν ὑπάρχειν, οὕτω καὶ τὸ ἔλαιον. ἐμπλάττει μὲν γάρ πως, μᾶλλον δὲ ἐπέχει τὰς διαπνοὰς τοῦ σώματος, ὅταν ψυχρὸν προσφέρηται, διαφορεῖ δὲ καὶ λεπτύνει τὸ θερμόν. ἔνεστι δέ σοι τοῦτο κᾀπὶ τῶν ἑψημένων κρεῶν ἐν αὐτῷ μαθεῖν. ὁμοίως γὰρ αὐτὰ τοῖς προεωλισθεῖσιν ἐργάζεται ψαθυρὰ, κᾂν εὐθέως ἅμα τῷ τυθῆναι τὸ ζῶον ἕψῃς ἢ ταγηνίσῃς ἐν αὐτῷ. διὰ τοῦτο οὖν κᾀγὼ τὴν διὰ τοῦ ἐλαίου κατάντλησιν εἱλόμην ἱκανῶς θερμοῦ φεύγων ἀεὶ τὸ χλιαρὸν, ἔτι δὲ μᾶλλον τὸ ψυχρόν.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: προεωλισθεῖσιν

For just as the cold is most contrary 580 to such conditions, so the lukewarm is not completely beneficial. For we learned in the first book of On the Power of Simple Drugs that, just as water is of such a nature as to produce the most contrary effects according as it is hot or cold, so too is oil. For it in a way plasters over, or rather checks, the transpirations of the body when it is applied cold, whereas the hot disperses and thins. You can learn this also from meats boiled in it. For it makes them crumbly, just like those that have been kept beforehand until stale, even if you boil or fry them in it immediately upon the animal's being slaughtered. For this reason, then, I too have chosen the affusion with oil that is sufficiently hot, always avoiding the lukewarm, and still more the cold.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: προεωλισθεῖσιν, ἐμπλάττει

For just as cold is most contrary 580 to such dispositions, so the lukewarm is not entirely beneficial. For we learned in the first book of On the Power of Simple Drugs that, just as water by nature produces most contrary effects according as it is hot or cold, so too does oil. For when it is applied cold it in a way plasters up, or rather it holds back, the transpirations of the body, whereas hot oil disperses and thins. You can learn this also from meats boiled in it. For it makes them friable in the same way as meats that have been kept overnight beforehand, even if you boil or fry them in it immediately upon the slaughter of the animal. For this reason, then, I too chose the affusion with oil that is sufficiently hot, always avoiding the lukewarm, and still more the cold.

Greek (Kühn)

ὕδατος δὲ χρῆσιν ἐν ὅλῃ τῇ θεραπείᾳ φυλάττομαι μέχρι τοῦ μηδ’ ἅψασθαι τοῦ ἕλκους, ὥσθ’ ὅταν μὲν εὐθέως ἐν ἀρχῇ δέῃ τὸ αἷμα τοῦ τετρωμένου μέρους ἀποπλῦναι, δι’ ἐλαίου τοῦτο πράττειν· ὅταν δ’ ἤδη μετρίως ἔχοντες οἱ κάμνοντες λούωνται, μηδὲ τότε ἐμβάλλειν ἐπιτρέπειν αὐτοῖς εἰς τὸ ὕδωρ τὸν πεπονθότα τόπον, ὡς τὸ πολὺ γὰρ ἐν αὐταῖς ταῖς χερσὶν 581 οἱ νευρότρωτοι γίνονται, μέμνημαι δέ τινα τῶν οὕτω τετρωμένων, ἐπειδὴ τετάρτην ἡμέραν ἄγων ἀνώδυνος τελέως ἦν ἀφλέγμαντόν τε τὸ πεπονθὸς ἑώρα μόριον, ἐν κρύει πολλῷ προελθόντα τῆς οἰκίας ἐπί τινα πρᾶξιν ἀναγκαίαν, ἐν ᾗ διατρίψας ἐπὶ πλέον ἦλθεν οἴκαδε τεινόμενος τὸ κῶλον ὅλον ἄχρι τοῦ τραχήλου καὶ μεγάλης ὀδύνης αἰσθόμενος. ὡς δ’ ἀφικομένου τινὸς ἐπ’ ἐμὲ παραγενόμενος ὡς αὐτὸν ἐθεασάμην ἐν οἷς ἦν, ἀποῤῥῖψαι μὲν ἐκέλευσα τὸ κατὰ τὸ μόριον ἔλαιον ψυχρὸν ἐκ τῆς προόδου γεγονὸς, ἐλαίῳ δὲ θερμῷ καταντλήσας ὅλον τὸ κῶλον, εἶτα καὶ τοῦ τραχήλου τὸ κατ’ εὐθὺ μέρος, ἐλαιοβρεχέσιν ἐρίοις πυριάσας ἐπιθείς τε τὸ διὰ τοῦ εὐφορβίου φάρμακον ὑγρὸν ἅμα καστορίῳ, παρηγόρησα τὸν ἄνθρωπον αὐτίκα καὶ ὕπνου ἐπιγενομένου μέχρι τῆς ἑσπέρας ἐπέπαυτο πάντα τὰ συμπτώματα. περὶ παντὸς οὖν ποιεῖσθαι χρὴ θερμὸν διαμένειν τὸ ἔριον, εἴτ’ οὖν ἐλαίῳ μόνῳ βεβρεγμένον εἴτε καὶ ὀξελαίῳ τύχοι. γένοιτο δ’ ἂν τοῦτο περιβαλλομένων ἔξωθεν ἐρίων ἄλλων ξηρῶν, ἔνδον τε μένοντος, ὅταν ᾖ χειμὼν ἄχρι τῆς ε΄ ἢ τῆς ζ΄.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἀποῤῥῖψαι, νευρότρωτοι γίνονται

But I guard against the use of water throughout the whole treatment, to the point that it does not even touch the wound, so that, when right at the beginning it is necessary to wash the blood off the wounded part, one does this with oil; and when the patients, already in a moderate condition, take a bath, not even then does one permit them to put the affected place into the water—for in most cases those with wounded nerves receive the wound in the hands themselves. 581 And I remember one of those wounded in this way who, when he was on his fourth day and was completely free of pain and saw that the affected part was free of inflammation, went out of the house in great cold for some necessary business, and having spent a longer time at it, came home with the whole limb strained with tension as far as the neck and feeling great pain. When someone came to me and I arrived at his side and saw the state he was in, I ordered the oil on the part, which had become cold from his going out, to be wiped off, and having bathed the whole limb with hot oil, and then also the part of the neck directly in line with it, and having fomented it with oil-soaked wool and applied the liquid drug made with euphorbium together with castoreum, I soothed the man at once, and when sleep supervened, by evening all the symptoms had ceased. One must therefore make it a matter of the greatest importance that the wool remain warm, whether it happens to be soaked in oil alone or in oil-and-vinegar. This would come about if other dry wools are wrapped around on the outside and the patient remains indoors, when it is winter, until the fifth or the seventh day.

The use of water I avoid throughout the whole treatment, to the point of not even letting it touch the wound. For the most part those with wounded nerves are wounded in the hands themselves 581. I remember one of those wounded in this way who, since on the fourth day he was entirely free of pain and saw that the affected part was free of inflammation, went out of his house in severe cold on some necessary business. Having spent rather a long time over it, he came home with the whole limb tense right up to the neck and feeling great pain. When someone came to fetch me and I went to him and saw the state he was in, I ordered the oil on the part, which had grown cold from his going out, to be removed. I poured hot oil over the whole limb, and then also over the part of the neck in line with it, fomented with wool soaked in oil, and applied the liquid drug made with euphorbium together with castoreum. With that I soothed the man at once, and when sleep came on, all the symptoms had ceased by evening. ﻿ 582 ἡμέρας τοῦ κάμνοντος. ἐὰν γὰρ ἄχρι ταύτης μήτε φλεγμονὴ φανεῖταί τις, ἀνώδυνός τε παντάπασιν ᾖ καὶ μηδεμιᾶς αἰσθάνηται τάσεως, ἐν ἀκινδύνῳ λοιπὸν ἔσται. φροντὶς δ’ ἔστω σοι τοῦ μηδὲν οἰνῶδες ἐμφαίνεσθαι τῷ ὄξει, καθάπερ γε καὶ κατὰ τοὔλαιον, ὡς ἀπηλλάχθαι στύψεως ἁπάσης. μήτε οὖν τὸ καλούμενον ὠμοτριβὲς ἢ ὀμφάκινον ἔστω, διττῶς γὰρ ὀνομάζεται μήτε τὸ ἀπὸ τῆς Ἰβηρίας, ὃ καλοῦσι Σπανὸν, ἀλλὰ μηδ’ ἄλλο τι τῶν θαλλοὺς ἐλαίας ἐχόντων. ἄριστον οὖν ἔλαιόν ἐστι τὸ λεπτομερὲς, ὁποῖον ἐν Ἰταλίᾳ μὲν τὸ Σαβῖνόν ἐστιν, ἄλλοθι δὲ τῶν ὑπὸ Ῥωμαίοις ἐθνῶν, ὅσα μηδεμίαν ἐμφαίνει στύψιν.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: θαλλοὺς

582 day of the patient. For if up to this day no inflammation appears, and he is entirely free of pain and perceives no tension, he will thereafter be out of danger. And let it be a concern of yours that nothing wine-like shows itself in the vinegar, just as also in the case of the oil, so that it is free of all astringency. Let it therefore be neither the so-called omotribes or omphacine (for it is named in two ways), nor that from Iberia, which they call Spanish, nor indeed any other of those that contain olive shoots. The best oil, then, is the fine-parted one, such as in Italy the Sabine is, and elsewhere, among the nations under the Romans, all those oils that display no astringency.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: θαλλοὺς ἐλαίας ἐχόντων, οἰνῶδες

582 day of the patient. For if up to this day no inflammation appears, and he is entirely free of pain and perceives no tension, he will thereafter be out of danger. Let it be your concern that nothing winy shows itself in the vinegar, just as also in the case of the oil, so that it is free of all astringency. Let it therefore be neither the so-called omotribes (oil of unripe olives) or omphacine (for it is named in two ways), nor the oil from Iberia, which they call Spanish, nor indeed any other of those that contain shoots of the olive. The best oil, then, is the fine-particled kind, such as the Sabine is in Italy, and elsewhere, among the nations under the Romans, those that show no astringency.

Greek (Kühn)

ὁποῖον δέ τι πρᾶγμά ἐστιν ἔνδειξις λογικὴ καὶ ὅσον πλουσιώτερον ἐμπειρίας ψιλῆς ἄνευ μεθόδου, κᾀκ τῶνδε μαθήσῃ νευροτρώτων τε πολλοῖς πολλάκις ἐν ὁδοῖς τε καὶ κατὰ πλοῦν καὶ κατ’ ἀγρὸν, ἢ ἐν πόλει μὲν, ἀλλὰ πόῤῥω τῆς ἐμῆς οἰκίας παραγενόμενος, οὔτε τοῦ δι’ εὐφορβίου φαρμάκου παρόντος οὔτε τοῦ δι’ ὄξους, ἀλλ’ οὔτε ἄλλου τῶν ἐν ἐκείνῳ τῷ χωρίῳ κατὰ τύχην εὐπορηθέντων φαρμάκων ἐπιθεὶς τῷ τραύματι, διὰ παντὸς 583 ἔτυχον τοῦ σκοποῦ. κατ’ ἀγρὸν μὲν γὰρ ἐμαυτὸν οἶδα καὶ πρόπολιν πρόσφατον, ὑγρὰν καὶ λιπαρὰν, ἣν ὁ γεωργὸς εἶχεν, ἐπιθέντα κατὰ τοῦ τραύματος, καὶ ποτὲ ζύμην μόνην καὶ ποτὲ ἄμφω μίξαντα. χρὴ δὲ τὴν μὲν ζύμην ὅτι παλαιοτάτην εἶναι, τὴν πρόπολιν δὲ ἐναντίως ἔχειν αὐτῇ κατὰ χρόνον. εἰ δὲ εἴη παλαιοτέρα, μαλαττέσθω παρὰ τὸ πῦρ δι’ ἐλαίου ἢ ἐν ἡλίῳ. ἐπ’ ἄλλου δέ τινος ἀγροίκου τῇ ζύμῃ τὸν ὀπὸν τοῦ τιθυμάλλου μίξας ἐπέθηκα, πλησίον τῆς αὐλῆς αὐτοῦ θεασάμενος αὐτὸν πεφυκότα. κατέγνων γὰρ τῆς ζύμης ὡς προσφάτου. καὶ ποτὲ πρόπολιν μετ’ ἐλαίου τήξας ἀνέδευσα τῇ ζύμῃ, προφυράσας αὐτὴν ὄξει δριμυτάτῳ. κάλλιον δ’ ἐπὶ τῶν τοιούτων μίξεων τὸ ἔλαιον παλαιὸν εἶναι, δι’ ὀξυμέλιτός τε καὶ ἀλεύρου κυά μων ἢ ὀρόβων ἢ αἰρῶν ἢ ἐρεβίνθων ἢ θέρμων πικρῶν ἢ πάλης ἀλφίτων ἢ κριθῶν ἀλεύρου, οἷς κατὰ τὰς πόλεις κέχρημαι καταπλάσμασιν οὐ μόνον ἐπὶ τῶν ἤδη φλεγμαινόντων, ἀλλὰ καὶ κατ’ ἀρχὰς εὐθέως.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἀλλ’ οὔτε ἄλλου τῶν ἐν ἐκείνῳ τῷ χωρίῳ κατὰ τύχην εὐπορηθέντων φαρμάκων ἐπιθεὶς, δι’ ὀξυμέλιτός τε καὶ ἀλεύρου, πάλης ἀλφίτων

What sort of thing rational indication is, and how much richer it is than bare experience without method, you will learn also from the following. Having come to many persons wounded in the nerves, often on journeys and on a voyage and in the country, or in a city but far from my own house, when neither the drug made with euphorbium was at hand nor the one made with vinegar, nor any other, but applying to the wound one of the drugs that happened to be available in that place, I always 583 hit the mark. For in the country I know that I myself applied to the wound fresh propolis, moist and oily, which the farmer had, and sometimes leaven alone, and sometimes having mixed both. The leaven should be as old as possible, while the propolis should be the opposite of it with respect to age; but if it should be rather old, let it be softened by the fire with oil, or in the sun. In the case of another countryman I mixed the juice of spurge with the leaven and applied it, having seen it growing near his farmyard; for I had judged the leaven deficient as being fresh. And once I melted propolis with oil and worked it into the leaven, having first kneaded the latter with the sharpest vinegar. It is better in such mixtures that the oil be old. [Also those] made with oxymel and the meal of beans or of bitter vetch or of darnel or of chickpeas or of bitter lupins or the fine dust of barley groats or barley meal, which I have used in the cities as cataplasms not only on parts already inflamed, but also straightaway at the beginning.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἀλλ’ οὔτε ἄλλου (οὔτε contradicts ἐπιθεὶς; probably corrupt), δι’ ὀξυμέλιτός τε καὶ ἀλεύρου (loose syntax linking to the preceding sentence), πάλης ἀλφίτων, αἰρῶν

What sort of thing rational indication is, and how much richer it is than bare experience without method, you will learn from these cases too. Often, when I came upon many nerve-wounded people on roads, on voyages, in the countryside, or in the city but far from my house, and neither the drug made with euphorbium nor the one made with vinegar was at hand, I applied to the wound some other of the drugs that chanced to be available in that place (the text reads 'nor any other'), and I always 583 attained my aim. In the countryside, I know that I applied to the wound fresh propolis, moist and fatty, which the farmer had; sometimes leaven alone; and sometimes both mixed together. The leaven should be as old as possible, whereas the propolis should be the opposite of it in respect of age. If the propolis is rather old, let it be softened with oil by the fire or in the sun. In the case of another rustic, I mixed the juice of the spurge with the leaven and applied it, having seen the plant growing near his farmyard. I had judged the leaven deficient because it was fresh. And sometimes I melted propolis with oil and worked it into the leaven, having first kneaded the leaven with the sharpest vinegar. In such mixtures it is better for the oil to be old. Then there are the poultices made with oxymel and flour of beans, or of bitter vetch, or of darnel, or of chickpeas, or of bitter lupins, or with fine meal of barley-groats, or with barley flour. These are the poultices I have used in the cities, not only on parts already inflamed but also immediately at the outset.

Greek (Kühn)

ἐν ἀγρῷ δὲ καὶ πίτταν εὑρὼν ὑγρὰν παρὰ τέκτονι, 584 τῇ ζύμῃ μίξας ἐπέθηκα, καὶ παρ’ ἄλλῳ τὸ τῶν ὀρόβων ἄλευρον ἀναδεύσας αὐτῇ. μὴ παρόντος δὲ τούτου καὶ τῶν προειρημένων ὁτιοῦν ἔνεστι μιγνύειν. καὶ ὀξέλαιον δὲ μόνον ἀναλαβὼν ἐρίῳ θερμὸν ἐπέβαλον εὐθέως, ἄλλου μὴ παρόντος. ἐθάρσησα γὰρ τῷ ὄξει δριμεῖ τε ἱκανῶς ὄντι καὶ παλαιῷ. ἐφ’ ἑτέρου δὲ προκατεψυγμένου, διὰ τὸ πρὸ μιᾶς ὥρας ἢ καὶ πλείονος ἔτι χρόνου τεθλᾶσθαι τὸ νεῦρον, ἄχρι τῶν ἐπιτηδείων τί μοι κομισθησόμενον ἀνέμενον, ἐλαίῳ καταντλεῖσθαι τὸ μόριον κελεύσας. καὶ τότε προμίξας ὄξος, ἐπειδὴ τὸ περιέχον θερμότατον ἦν ὥρᾳ θέρους τὸ μικτὸν ἐξ ἀμφοῖν ἄχρι πλείστου κατήντλουν, ἕως τῶν ἄλλων ἐκομίσθη τι φαρμάκων. ἕκαστον δὲ τῶν εἰρημένων ἀλεύρων καὶ τῆς στακτῆς διαμίξας πολλάκις, ἐπέπλασα καὶ κατ’ ἀρχὰς εὐθέως καὶ μετὰ ταῦτα φθάσαντος ἀνθρώπου τῶν ἐναίμων τινὶ φαρμάκων οὐ καλῶς κεχρῆσθαι. πάντα δ’ οὖν ταῦτα τὰ ὁπωσοῦν ἐπιτιθέμενα φάρμακα θερμὰ ταῖς ποιότησι προσφερέσθω.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: στακτῆς

And in the country, having also found liquid pitch at a carpenter's, 584 I mixed it with leaven and applied it; and at another man's I applied bitter-vetch flour, having kneaded it up with the leaven. And if this is not available, one may mix in any whatever of the things mentioned before. And I have also applied immediately oxelaeum alone, hot, taken up in wool, when nothing else was available; for I had confidence in the vinegar, which was sufficiently sharp and old. In the case of another man who had already been chilled beforehand, because the nerve had been bruised an hour or even a still longer time earlier, I waited until one of the suitable things should be brought to me, having ordered that the part be bathed with oil; and then, having first mixed in vinegar, since the surrounding air was very hot, it being the season of summer, I kept bathing it with the mixture of both for as long as possible, until one of the other drugs was brought. And often, having mixed each of the flours mentioned with stacte, I applied it as a plaster both right at the beginning and afterwards, when the person had previously made bad use of one of the drugs for bleeding wounds. All these drugs, then, applied in whatever way, should be administered warm in their qualities.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: στακτῆς, παρ’ ἄλλῳ, προκατεψυγμένου, θερμὰ ταῖς ποιότησι

In the country, too, having found liquid pitch at a carpenter's, 584 I mixed it with the leaven and applied it; and with another patient I kneaded the meal of bitter vetch into it. If this is not at hand, it is possible to mix in any one whatever of the aforementioned as well. I have also taken up oxelaeum (vinegar-and-oil) alone in wool and applied it warm at once, when nothing else was at hand; for I had confidence in the vinegar, since it was both sufficiently sharp and old. In another case, where the part had already been chilled beforehand because the nerve had been bruised an hour or even still longer before, I waited until something of the suitable remedies should be brought to me, having ordered the part to be bathed with oil poured over it. And then, having first mixed in vinegar, since the ambient air was very hot, it being the summer season, I kept pouring the mixture of both over it for a very long time, until some one of the other drugs was brought. Having often mixed each of the aforesaid meals with the percolated lye, I applied it as a poultice, both right at the beginning and afterwards, when the person had already made improper use of one of the 'bloody' (enaimos) drugs. At any rate, let all these drugs, in whatever way they are applied, be administered hot in their qualities.

Greek (Kühn)

καὶ μέντοι κᾀπὸ τῆς ἐμαυτοῦ ποτ’ οἰκίας, οὐδενὸς τῶν συνθέτων φαρμάκων ἑτοίμου εὑρισκομένου, σπεύδων προελθεῖν, 585 ἔδωκά τινι τῶν ἐμῶν παλαιὸν εὐφόρβιον, ἐκέλευσά τε λειώσαντα μετ’ ἐλαίου παλαιοῦ, μέχρι πάχους ὑγρᾶς κηρωτῆς, ἐπιθεῖναι τῷ νύγματι τοῦ κατὰ τὴν χεῖρα τένοντος. ὡς δ’ ἐπανελθὼν ἐπυνθανόμην τοῦ τρωθέντος, εἰ θερμασίας πολλῆς αἴσθησις αὐτῷ γίγνοιτο κατὰ τὸν τόπον, ὁ δὲ συμμέτρου τινὸς ἔφησεν αἰσθάνεσθαι. πάλιν οὖν ἐπυθόμην εἰ δάκνοιτο κατὰ τὸ τραῦμα πρὸς τοῦ φαρμάκου. τοῦ δὲ εἰπόντος μὴ δάκνεσθαι μὲν, οἱονεὶ δὲ κνήσεώς τινος αἴσθησιν ἔχειν, συνεχώρησα μέχρι τῆς νυκτὸς ἐπικεῖσθαι τὸ φάρμακον. ἐπεὶ δὲ ἀφαιρεθέντων τῶν ἐπικειμένων θερμασία τέ μοι σύμμετρος ἐφαίνετο κατὰ τὸ πεπονθὸς χωρίον καὶ μὴ μεμυκὸς τὸ νύγμα, κάλλιον ἔδοξέ μοι εἶναι τῷ αὐτῷ χρῆσθαι φαρμάκῳ. καὶ μέντοι κατὰ τὴν ὑστεραίαν ἀμέμπτως ἔχοντος τοῦ νευροτρώτου καὶ κατὰ τὴν τρίτην τε καὶ τετάρτην ἄχρι τέλους ἐχρησάμην τῷ αὐτῷ φαρμάκῳ, καί τις τοῦτο θεασάμενος ἐφ’ ἑτέρου τρωθέντος ὁμοίως ἐχρήσατο τῷ προσφάτῳ εὐφορβίῳ κατὰ τὸν αὐτὸν τόπον.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ὁμοίως

And indeed once, from my own house as well, when none of the compound drugs was found ready to hand and I was in haste to go out, 585 I gave one of my people old euphorbium and ordered him to triturate it with old oil to the thickness of a liquid cerate and to apply it to the puncture of the tendon in the hand. When I returned and inquired of the wounded man whether he had a sensation of much heat in the place, he said that he perceived a moderate degree. So I asked again whether he was being bitten at the wound by the drug. When he said that he was not being bitten, but had a sensation as it were of some itching, I allowed the drug to remain on until night. And since, once the applications had been removed, the heat in the affected region appeared to me moderate and the puncture had not closed up, it seemed to me better to use the same drug. And indeed, since the man wounded in the nerve was faring faultlessly on the following day, and on the third and the fourth as well, I used the same drug through to the end; and someone who saw this, in the case of another man who had been wounded, used in the same way fresh euphorbium on the same place.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: τινι τῶν ἐμῶν, κατὰ τὸν αὐτὸν τόπον

Moreover, once also, from my own house, when none of the compound drugs was found ready to hand and I was hurrying to go out, 585 I gave one of my people some old euphorbium, and ordered him to grind it with old oil to the consistency of a liquid cerate and apply it to the puncture of the tendon in the hand. When I returned and asked the wounded man whether he had a sensation of much heat in the place, he said he felt a moderate one. So I asked him again whether he was being bitten at the wound by the drug. When he said that he was not bitten, but had the sensation as it were of some itching, I allowed the drug to remain applied until night. And when, after what had been applied was removed, the heat in the affected region appeared to me moderate and the puncture had not closed, it seemed to me better to use the same drug. And indeed, since on the following day the man with the wounded nerve was in an irreproachable condition, on the third and fourth days too, right to the end, I used the same drug; and someone who had seen this used fresh euphorbium in the same way on another man wounded in the same place.

Greek (Kühn)

ἐξ ἀνάγκης οὖν ἠκολούθησε τῷ τετρωμένῳ θερμασία τε πολλὴ καὶ δῆξις καὶ ὀδύνη κατὰ τὸ ἕλκος, ὥστε ἀχθῆναι πρὸς ἐμὲ 586 τὸν ἄνθρωπον, ὡς ἐπὶ παραδόξῳ τῷ συμβεβηκότι. κᾀγὼ συνεὶς αὐτίκα τοῦ γεγονότος ἀπέῤῥιψα μὲν τὰ ἐπικείμενα, καταντλήσας δ’ αὐτὸν ἐλαίῳ τὸ διὰ τοῦ ὄξους ἐπέθηκα φάρμακον. εἰ δ’ οὐκ ἦν τοῦθ’ ἕτοιμον, ἐπετεθείκειν ἂν τὸ διὰ εὐφορβίου, πλέονα κηρὸν ἔχον. οὐκοῦν ἐχρῆν, καθάπερ ὀλίγον ἔμπροσθεν ἐλέγομεν μὴ ἀγνοεῖν τάς τε δυνάμεις καὶ τὰς ἰδέας τῶν δοκίμων φαρμάκων, οὐδὲ τίνα μὲν ἐν τάχει μεταβάλλει τὴν δύναμιν, τίνα δὲ ἕως παμπόλλου διαφυλάττει. τῶν γὰρ ταχέως ἐξίτηλον ἐχόντων τὴν θερμότητα τὸ εὐφόρβιόν ἐστι· γνωριεῖς δὲ αὐτὸ καὶ χωρὶς τοῦ διαμασήσασθαι τὸ χρῶμα θεασάμενος. οὐ γὰρ ὡς ἐν ἀρχῇ τεφρῶδες, ἀλλ’ ὕπωχρόν τε καὶ ὑπόξανθον γίνεται παλαιούμενον. εὐθὺς δὲ καὶ ξηρὸν ἱκανῶς φαίνεται τηνικαῦτα καὶ διαλύεται μόγις ἐν τῷ λειοῦσθαι δι’ ἐλαίου τοῦ νέου ταχέως χυλουμένου. χρὴ δ’ οὐκ εὐθέως αὐτὸ μιγνύναι τῷ ἐλαίῳ, ἀλλὰ πρότερον ἐν τῇ θυείᾳ λειοῦν ἀτρέμα, ὅπως μὴ πλατυνθῇ.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: πλατυνθῇ, χυλουμένου

Of necessity, then, there followed for the wounded man much heat and bitingness and pain at the wound, so that the man was brought to me 586, as over an occurrence that was contrary to expectation. And I, understanding at once what had happened, threw off what had been applied, and having fomented him with oil I applied the drug made with vinegar. But if this had not been ready, I would have applied the one made with euphorbium, containing more wax. So one ought, as we were saying a little earlier, not to be ignorant of both the powers and the forms of the approved drugs, nor of which ones change their power quickly and which preserve it for a very long time. For euphorbium is among those whose heat quickly fades; and you will recognise it even without chewing it, by looking at the colour. For as it ages it becomes not ash-coloured, as at the beginning, but somewhat pale and somewhat yellowish. And at the same time it also appears then quite dry and dissolves only with difficulty when it is triturated with oil, whereas the fresh quickly turns to juice. But one must not mix it with the oil straightaway, but first triturate it gently in the mortar, so that it is not flattened out.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: καταντλήσας δ’ αὐτὸν, χυλουμένου, πλατυνθῇ, ὕπωχρόν

Of necessity, then, great heat, biting and pain in the wound ensued in the wounded man, so that the man was brought to me 586, as though for something unexpected that had befallen him. And I, understanding at once what had happened, threw off what had been applied, and after pouring oil over him I applied the drug made with vinegar. Had this not been at hand, I would have applied the drug made with euphorbium that contains more wax. So then, just as we were saying a little earlier that one must not be ignorant of the potencies and the forms of approved drugs, so too one must not be ignorant of which of them change their potency quickly and which preserve it for a very long time. For euphorbium is one of those whose hotness quickly fades. You will recognise this even without chewing it, by looking at its colour. For as it grows old it becomes not ash-coloured, as it is at first, but somewhat pale and somewhat yellow. At the same time it then also appears quite dry, and it dissolves only with difficulty when it is ground with oil, whereas the fresh is quickly reduced to juice. One must not mix it with the oil straight away, but first grind it gently in the mortar, so that it is not flattened out.

And what need is there to say that, when you taste it too, the fresh 587 appears most fiery, so as to burn the tongue, whereas the old produces little heat? Now it happens to many other drugs as well that their power becomes faded within a few years, but to none less than euphorbium, after thapsia at any rate. For the latter becomes quite weak after a year, and expires completely once it has become two years old. Euphorbium, however, when it is good from the beginning and is well stored, still preserves some power even in the third year, and sometimes even in the fourth; but it is already losing its strength in the fifth and sixth, and then, as time advances, it is completely extinguished. The one which I said I once used with oil, aged, without wax, was about five or six years old. Hence it is not even possible to comprehend its use within a fixed measure. For when it is new it is mixed with ten and twelve times as much oil together with three times as much wax; but as it grows old one must put in more of it; and when it has become three or four years old, either double the initial amount or an amount equal to the wax.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἀκόποις

So whenever you wish the 588 drug to have a consistency similar to the unguents called akopa, which indeed is also that of the liquid cerate, the oil must be four times the wax. But when it is to become plaster-like, one must as a rule mix the wax in equal amount to the oil, especially if the wax is dry and old, since wax that is fresh and fatty requires less oil for making a plaster-like cerate, and still more so in the summer season. So someone wishing to make the cerate plaster-like, having melted the wax in oil according to the proportion I have stated, will mix into it a sixth part of fresh euphorbium, so that there is one drachm of the latter, six of wax, and six or five of oil. But since the glutinous and viscous quality does not belong to wax, as it does to resin and to pitch, for this reason I mix into the cerate resin or pitch or a portion of each. It is quite evident, then, that when these are mixed in, the oil is no longer to be put in equal to them. For wax requires a great deal of the fatty matter in order to become liquid, whereas among resins the dry ones need altogether little, while the liquid ones even want to take on some dry substance in order to reach a plaster-like consistency.

Now, whenever you wish the drug to have a consistency similar to the ointments called acopa, 588 which is indeed also the consistency of the liquid cerate, the oil must be four times the wax. For wax needs much fat for moistness, whereas of the resins the dry ones need altogether little, and the moist ones even want to take on some dry substance besides for a plaster-like consistency.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: κεραμείων, φύσημα

In 589 the waxes, then, there is no great difference in respect of moistness and dryness, whereas in the resins, conversely, as also in pitch, the difference between the dry ones and the moist is very great. The driest of the resins, then, is the one which some call roasted (phrykte), others colophony; second is the one from the jars (kerameia), impure and unroasted, but if one purifies it, it passes over into the roasted kind. Drier than both of these is what is called pine-blister (pityinon physema). This one, inasmuch as it is parched, I judged not suitable for the composition of the plaster under discussion and rejected, but I made use of the roasted one and the moist ones. And of these too some remain moist for a longer time, like the terebinth, while some dry quickly, like the strobilos (pine-cone) resin. Intermediate between both is the fir resin. And indeed in respect of their powers too the strobilos resin is hotter, the fir resin second, and the terebinth third after both. As for the cypress resin, I was wary of mixing it in, since it has something of astringency.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: πιτύϊνον φύσημα, ἐκ τῶν κεραμείων, ὑπώπτευσα μὴ μιγνύειν

589 Among waxes, then, there is no great difference with respect to moistness and dryness; with resins, by contrast, as also with pitch, the difference between the dry ones and the moist ones is very great. The driest of the resins is the one which some call 'roasted' and others 'Colophonian'. Second is the one from the potteries, unpurified and unroasted; but if one purifies it, it passes over into the roasted kind. Drier than both of these is the so-called pine physēma (blister-resin). But someone will think that Dioscorides of Anazarbus disagrees with us in the account of resins, since he wrote the following statement in the 590 first book On Materials (περὶ ὕλης): "Of all the resins the terebinth takes first place, and after it the mastic (schinine), then the pine (pityine) and the fir (elatine), after which are counted the peuke-pine (peukine) and the strobilos (pine-cone) resin." For in these words Dioscorides appears to hold the strobilos resin to be the poorest and the terebinth the best. But I, of these three resins — strobilos, fir, terebinth — said that the strobilos is first and best in respect of heat, the fir second, and the terebinth last. Just as, then, one who says that of the austere wines in Italy the best is the Italian wine [speaks truly] — for the one makes the reference of his statement to a single quality, while the other pronounces simply about their whole substance, and (each) speaks truly — in the same way in the case of the resins too one might reasonably say that the terebinth is the best, as a drug of the most manifold uses (polychreston), yet not that it holds first place in heat.

Someone will think that we disagree, in our account of resins, with Dioscorides of Anazarbus, who wrote the following statement in the 590 first book On Materials: 'The terebinth takes precedence over all the resins, and after it the mastic resin, then the pine resin and the fir resin, after which are counted the peuce resin and the strobiline.' For in these words Dioscorides evidently regards the strobiline as the worst and the terebinth as the best. But I, of these three resins, strobiline, fir and terebinth, said that the strobiline is first and best in respect of heat, the fir resin second, and the terebinth last. Just as, then, the one who says that of the austere wines in Italy the Italian wine is best speaks the truth (for the one refers his statement to a single quality, while the other pronounces simply about their whole substance), in the same way also with the resins one might reasonably say that the terebinth is the best, as being a most polychrest drug, but not that it ranks first in heat.
