Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἣ δι’ ἐλαίου τε καὶ κηροῦ μόνον συντίθεμεν

As I was saying, then, it is possible for you, by mixing with the wax whichever of the resins you wish, to bring the simple cerate, which we compound from oil and wax 591 alone, to a more adhesive consistency. In this matter there is also need of trial, to judge our conjecture. For having mixed three times as much wax with the terebinth, you may determine whether the drug has already taken on a plaster-like consistency, or whether it is still rather dry and needs yet more resin; so that whenever, as you make trial in this way, it appears to you to be in due proportion, then you mix into the drug that is being made the twelfth part of euphorbium. But if you do not know whether the euphorbium is exactly hot, or falls somewhat short of being such, then after the preparation spread a little of what has been prepared on a linen cloth and apply it to your own shin or forearm for some hours; then, if it seems to you to heat moderately, suppose that what has been prepared is well-tempered. But if it either produces no perceptible heat or is more fiery than is fitting, it is possible either to relax the drug or to intensify it: to relax it by admixture of cerate, to intensify it by the addition of euphorbium.

As I was saying, then, by mixing with the wax whichever of the resins you wish, you can bring the simple cerate, which we compound from oil and wax 591 alone, to a more adhesive consistency. In this, however, there is also need of experience, to judge our conjecture. For you can mix a threefold quantity of the wax with the terebinth and learn whether the drug has already taken on a plaster-like consistency, or whether it is still rather too dry and needs still more resin. Then, if it seems to you to heat moderately, take the preparation to be well tempered. But if it either produces no perceptible heating or is more fiery than is fitting, you can either weaken the drug or intensify it: weaken it by mixing in cerate, intensify it by adding euphorbium.

Greek (Kühn)

ἐάν γε μὴν λιπαρὰν ἔχῃς πρόπολιν, ἐπιτηδειοτέραν ἴσθι σοι πασῶν τῶν ῥητινῶν ἐσομένην αὐτὴν, ὡς ἂν ἐπισπᾶσθαί τε ﻿ 592 καὶ τοῦ βάθους ἕλκειν ἔξω πεφυκυῖαν ἀτμώδη τε πάχη πνεύματος, ὑγρότητάς τε περιττάς. αἱ μὲν γὰρ ῥητίναι μετὰ τοῦ κηροῦ τῆς ἐμπλαστρώδους συστάσεως ἕνεκεν, οὐχ ὡς ὑπὸ τὸν σκοπὸν τῆς θεραπείας πεπτωκυῖαι παραλαμβάνονται. τοῦ σκοποῦ δ’ αὐτοῦ τῆς ἰάσεως ἡ πρόπολις ἔχεται. καί ποτε αὐτῇ μόνῃ προσφάτῳ καθ’ ὑγρὸν ἐχρησάμην, ὡς ἔφην, ἐπί τινος γεωργοῦ σκληρὸν ἔχοντος σῶμα, καὶ θαυμαστὸν ὅπως ὤνητο μόνῃ χρησάμενος ἄχρι τέλους, ὡς μηδενὸς δεηθῆναι φαρμάκου, βαθύτερον ἔχοντος ἑτέρου τὸ νύγμα βραχύ τι προσέμιξα τῶν τιθυμάλλων τινὸς ὀποῦ. τοῖς μέντοι μαλακοῖς σώμασι σκληρότερον φαίνεται τὸ φάρμακον ἐν τῇ ψαύσει, πλὴν εἰ πάνυ τις αὐτὸ μαλακύνει ἐλαίου μίξει. καὶ μέντοι καὶ τἄλλα φάρμακα πάνθ’ ὅσα προσφέρεται τοῖς νευροτρώτοις, ὑγρὰ καὶ μαλακὰ προσῆκεν εἶναι. τὴν μὲν οὖν μαλακότητα, πᾶν ἔλαιον δύναται παρασχεῖν, τὴν δ’ ἰσχὺν τοῦ φαρμάκου φυλάξαι τὸ παλαιὸν ἔλαιον.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: καθ’ ὑγρὸν, βαθύτερον ἔχοντος ἑτέρου τὸ νύγμα

If, however, you have oily propolis, know that it will be more suitable for you than all the resins, inasmuch as it is of a nature to attract 592 and draw out from the depth vaporous thicknesses of pneuma and superfluous moistures. For the resins, together with the wax, are taken up for the sake of the plaster-like consistency, not as falling under the aim of the treatment; but propolis pertains to the very aim of the cure. And once I used it alone, fresh, in a moist state, as I said, on a certain farmer who had a hard body, and it was wonderful how he benefited, using it alone until the end, so that he needed no drug at all; when another man had a deeper puncture, I mixed in a little of the juice of one of the spurges. To soft bodies, however, the drug appears rather hard to the touch, unless one softens it thoroughly by an admixture of oil. And indeed all the other drugs too, as many as are applied to those wounded in the nerves, ought to be moist and soft. Now the softness any oil is able to provide, but it is old oil that preserves the strength of the drug.

If, however, you have greasy propolis, know that it will be more suitable for you than all the resins, since it is by nature apt both to draw in 592 and to pull out from the depth vaporous thicknesses of pneuma and superfluous moistures. For the resins are taken together with the wax for the sake of the plaster-like consistency, not as falling under the aim of the treatment; but propolis pertains to the very aim of the cure. And once, as I said, I used it alone, fresh, in liquid form, in the case of a certain farmer who had a hard body, and it was wonderful how he benefited, using it alone to the end, so that he needed no other drug; in the case of another, who had the puncture deeper, I mixed in a little of the juice of one of the spurges. To soft bodies, however, the drug appears rather hard to the touch, unless one softens it thoroughly by mixture of oil. And indeed all the other drugs that are applied to the nerve-wounded ought to be liquid and soft. Softness, then, any oil can provide, but old oil can preserve the strength of the drug.

And just as I once, in the country, applied propolis, having selected it as the better drug among those then available, so too I applied leaven on another occasion 593 in the case of another patient, when no drug was at hand, since I knew that this too draws out what lies in the depth and disperses it. And the longer the time the leaven has been kept, the better it is. Not only are such things readily found in country districts, but also unburnt sulphur, which I myself also once used, mixing it with the tetrapharmakon. With this, then, I also often used, having mixed them in, aphronitron and Berenician nitron and aphrolitron—not the stony kind, but genuine aphrolitron, which has a foamy consistency—not only in the country for lack of the others, but also when the aforementioned drugs were available, for the sake of testing my devising by experience; and through such use alone the wounded man was cured, being, of course, fomented with the oil. For let this be a common rule for you in the case of all the drugs here said to be suitable for those wounded in the nerves. And also that which is called arsenikon by physicians, but arrhenikon by those who wish to Atticize everything, I mixed with the tetrapharmakos preparation, wishing to make trial of my devising.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

And again, when this was not available, 594 [I mixed in] what is called sandarach, just as also lime, both slaked and unslaked, both unwashed and washed, putting in a little of the unslaked, more of the slaked, and still more of the washed. The exact quantity in each composition will be stated a little later, once I have first gone through all these simples and the distinctions governing their use. For in this discourse, on account of the Empiricists, who think that most of the compound drugs have been discovered by chance encounter, it seemed good to me to go through the treatment of those wounded in the nerves, which was discovered by me without chance encounter, from knowing the aim of their therapy and understanding the powers of the simple drugs. For by chance encounter some one of the simple drugs is discovered, whereas the rational method discovers many of the simples all at once, and very many compositions on the basis of them. At any rate, all those that are of such a nature as to draw outward from the depth and to disperse the moistures are suitable for those wounded in the nerves.

Rendering B (opus-5-5)

And again, when this was not at hand, 594 [I mixed in] the so-called realgar (sandarachē), just as I also did with lime (titanos), both slaked and unslaked, both unwashed and washed, putting in a little of the unslaked, but more of the slaked, and still more of the washed. The exact quantity for each composition will be stated a little later, once I have first gone through all these simples and the distinctions (diorismoi) governing their use. For in this discourse it seemed good to me to set out the treatment (agōgē) of the nerve-wounded because of the Empiricists, who hold that most compound drugs have been discovered by accidental encounter (periptōsis). This treatment was discovered by me without accidental encounter, from knowing the aim (skopos) of their therapy and from understanding the powers (dynameis) of the simple drugs. For by accidental encounter a single one of the simple drugs is found, whereas the rational (logikē) method finds many of the simples all together, and a great many compositions based on them. At any rate, all things whose nature is to draw outward from the depth and to disperse (diaphorein) the moistures are suitable for the nerve-wounded.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: χωρὶς τοῦ μὴ βλάπτειν, ἐπ’ αὐτῆς τῆς δευτέρας

But since some of these have, coupled with them, heats that are rather immoderate, or strongly biting acridities or sharpnesses, it is reasonable to mix with them 595 other drugs capable of rendering the aforesaid excesses more moderate without harming the power. But it is not possible to do this correctly without knowing, for each of the drugs, not only the kind of its quality but also the quantity within it. For some, as we showed in the treatise On Simple Drugs, are of the first order of heating things, having departed a little from the well-tempered; others are in the second itself, standing further away; and some are of the third and fourth, certain others being already able to burn. Accordingly, in proportion to the vehemence of each drug in respect of its power, the power of the things mixed together is discovered, if one knows the temperaments of all the simple drugs. But the method of the Empiricists, which mixes only things of the same kind, comes about both through ignorance of the nature of each drug and, for those who do know this, through the quantity of the excess not being securely known.

I myself, then, composed the drug which I first prepared out of many homogeneous ingredients, because I did not yet know precisely the quantity 596 of the power of each. 597 The liquid drugs, then, are less distressing, both for other wounds and especially for those in the nerves. But we also use the plaster-like ones because of those who wish to bathe. For once the wool has been removed, having only such a drug itself lying upon them, they bathe with less disturbance. When, however, the wound has occurred in the foot, it is impossible not to wet the part, but one must at that time abstain from the bath for the first four days. But since in present-day life endurance has perished among all men, so that by now even those in the fields have become accustomed to bathe every day, we have those who are not very luxurious—and these would be the poor—obedient for the most part, but the rich, and especially those of them who have great power, either hard to persuade or entirely disobedient. The best course, then, would have been not even to treat them.

ἐπεὶ δ’ οὐκ οἶδ’ ὅπως κατ’ ἄλλην ἄλλος αἰτίαν οἱ μὲν ἄκοντες ἡμῶν, οἱ δὲ ἑκόντες ἀναγκάζονται τῆς μαλακίας αὐτῶν ἀνέχεσθαι, 598 τοιοῦτόν τι ποιεῖν εἴωθα. κατὰ τοῦ τετρωμένου μέρους ἐπιτιθεὶς τῶν ἐμπλαστρωδῶν τι φαρμάκων, ἔξωθεν αὐτοῦ πάλιν ἐπιβάλλω πολυπτύχους ὀθόνας πλέονας ἐλαίῳ διαβρόχους, αἷς αὖθις ἔξωθεν ἐπίδεσμον ἐξ ὀθόνης ἐπιβάλλω, καὶ μετὰ ταῦτα πάλιν ἐν αὐτῷ τῷ βαλανείῳ προκαταχέω τὸ ἔλαιον τοῦ ἐπιδέσμου, μέλλοντος εἰς τὴν πτύελον ἐμβαίνειν τοῦ νευροτρώτου καὶ δηλονότι λουσαμένου, τὰ μὲν ἐπικείμενα πάντα ἀποβάλλω, χρῶμαι δὲ τῇ προειρημένη τῆς ὅλης θεραπείας ἀγωγῇ. εὔδηλον δ’ ὅτι καὶ τῆς τῶν μορίων φύσεως ἔμπειρον εἶναι χρὴ τὸν μέλλοντα καλῶς προνοήσασθαι τῶν νευροτρώτων. εἴρηται γὰρ ἔμπροσθεν ὅτι καὶ οἱ τένοντες, εἰς οὓς οἱ πλεῖστοι τῶν μυῶν τελευτῶσι, συμπαθείας οὐ μικρὰς ἐπιφέρουσι, πρῶτον μὲν τοὺς σπασμοὺς γεννῶντες, ὕστερον δὲ σηπόμενοί τε αὐτοὶ καὶ τὰ πλησιάζοντα πάντα συνδιαφθείροντες. ἔνιοι δ’ αὐτῶν ὡς ὑμένες εἰσὶ λεπτοὶ καὶ πλατεῖς, ὁποῖοι κατά τε τὰ πρὸς τοῖς γόνασι τέλη τῶν μηρῶν ἔνδον τε τῶν χειρῶν καὶ κάτω τῶν ποδῶν ὑποτέτανται.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: πτύελον

But since, I know not how, each for a different reason, some of us unwillingly and others willingly are compelled to put up with their softness, 598 I am accustomed to do something of this sort. Placing one of the plaster-like drugs upon the wounded part, I apply over it on the outside in turn several many-folded linen cloths soaked in oil, and over these again, on the outside, I apply a bandage of linen cloth; and after this, once more, in the bath itself I first pour the oil over the bandage, when the person wounded in the nerves is about to step into the tub; and obviously, when he has bathed, I remove everything that lies upon the part and employ the previously described regimen of the whole treatment. And it is quite evident that one who is going to take proper care of those wounded in the nerves must also be experienced in the nature of the parts. For it was said earlier that the tendons too, in which most of the muscles terminate, bring about no small sympathetic affections, first generating spasms, and later both putrefying themselves and destroying along with themselves all the neighbouring parts. Some of them are thin and broad like membranes, such as those which are stretched beneath at the ends of the thighs by the knees, on the inside of the hands, and on the underside of the feet.

But since, I know not how, each for a different reason, some of us unwillingly and others willingly are compelled to put up with their softness, 598 I am accustomed to do something of this kind. Placing one of the plaster-like drugs on the wounded part, I again lay over it on the outside several many-folded linen cloths soaked through with oil, and over these in turn, on the outside, I put a bandage of linen cloth. After this, again in the bath itself, I first pour the oil over the bandage when the nerve-wounded patient is about to step into the tub [reading πύελον for πτύελον]; and when he has, of course, bathed, I take off everything that was laid on and follow the previously described course of the whole treatment. It is quite clear that whoever is going to take proper care of the nerve-wounded must also be experienced in the nature of the parts. For it was said earlier that the tendons too, in which most of the muscles terminate, bring on no small sympathetic affections, first producing spasms, and later both putrefying themselves and destroying along with themselves everything that lies near. Some of them are like membranes, thin and broad, such as are stretched underneath at the ends of the thighs next to the knees, on the inner side of the hands, and on the underside of the feet.

For all these touch the skin, 599 those of them that are in the hands and the feet being quite firmly grown together with it, whereas those that run down to the kneecap are set free from the surrounding skin by flaying (κατὰ δάρσιν). And this too befell me by chance: for I devised the regimen of treatment for those wounded in the nerves while still young, when I first returned from Alexandria to my native city, being in my twenty-eighth year. Rendering A (fable-5-1)

For whereas all the physicians before me 600 both fomented them for a very long time with hot water and applied poultices of wheaten flour moderately boiled in water-and-oil (hydrelaion), I did not apply the water at all, but, bathing them frequently with the oil together with the drugs mentioned, I quickly rendered them all healthy. And since by chance many had died in the preceding years, whereas under me not one of those wounded in the manner described died, nor anyone from any other wound, the second high priest after the one who had then entrusted the treatment to me likewise himself entrusted to me the care of the gladiators, after an interval of seven months. For the first held the high-priesthood around the autumnal equinox, the second at the height of spring. And when again under this one all were saved, the third and the fourth and the fifth likewise entrusted to me the treatment of the gladiators, so that I have had an extensive testing of the method of treatment. And indeed in this period something of the following kind was also devised by me, and those physicians who wish to handle the treatment of wounds correctly must pay particular attention to it.

θεασάμενος 601 γάρ τινα μονομάχων τῶν καλουμένων ἱππέων ἐγκαρσίαν ἔχοντα διαίρεσιν, ἄχρι βάθους πολλοῦ κατὰ τὰ πρόσω τε καὶ κάτω τοῦ μηροῦ καὶ τὸ χεῖλος ἰδὼν τοῦ τραύματος, ἄνωθεν μὲν τὸ ἕτερον ἀνεσπασμένον, κάτω δὲ θάτερον, ὡς ἐπὶ τὴν μύλην κατεσπασμένον, ἐτόλμησα τῆς κατὰ πλάτος ὀνομαζομένης ἀγωγῆς ἀποστὰς, συναγαγεῖν ῥαφαῖς πρὸς ἄλληλα τὰ διεστῶτα μέρη τῶν μυῶν. εὐλαβηθεὶς οὖν συνδιαῤῥάπτειν αὐτοῖς τοὺς τένοντας, ἀποδείρας πρότερον ἐκείνους, οὕτως ἐχρησάμην διὰ πολλοῦ βάθους ταῖς ῥαφαῖς, εἰδὼς ἀκίνδυνον μὲν εἰς αὐτὸ τὸ σαρκῶδες μυῶν, τὸν κίνδυνον δὲ ἐκ τῶν τενόντων γινόμενον, οὐδὲ τοῦτό τινα τῶν διδασκάλων ἑωρακὼς ποιοῦντα. τινὲς μὲν γὰρ αὐτῶν ἐν ταῖς τοιαύταις τρώσεσι τὰ τοῦ δέρματος χείλη μόνα συνέῤῥαπτον, ἔνιοι δὲ κατὰ τὰ σαρκώδη τοὺς μῦς αὐτοὺς τολμῶντες ῥάπτειν, ἐπιπολῆς αὐτῶν ἐποιοῦντο τὴν συναγωγὴν, ὥστε εἴ ποτε μέχρι βάθους συχνοῦ τὸ τραῦμα διῆκον ἐγεγόνει μὴ κατὰ τὸ μῆκος τοῦ κώλου, τὸ μὲν ἐπιπολῆς τοῦ μυὸς κολλᾶσθαι, τὸ δ’ ἄλλο πᾶν ἀκόλλητον μένειν.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: τῆς κατὰ πλάτος ὀνομαζομένης ἀγωγῆς, μύλην, ἀποδείρας

For having observed 601 one of the gladiators called horsemen (hippeis) who had a transverse division, extending to a great depth, in the front and lower part of the thigh, and having seen the lip of the wound—the one drawn up above, the other drawn down below, as if toward the kneecap (mylē)—I ventured, departing from the treatment named 'according to breadth' (kata platos), to bring together with sutures the separated parts of the muscles toward one another. Taking care, then, not to sew the tendons together along with them, having first stripped those away, I thus employed the sutures through a great depth, knowing that it is free of danger into the fleshy part itself of the muscles, whereas the danger arises from the tendons—and this too I had not seen any of my teachers doing. For some of them, in wounds of this kind, used to sew together only the lips of the skin, while some, venturing to sew the muscles themselves in their fleshy parts, made the bringing-together of them at the surface, so that if ever the wound had come to extend to a considerable depth, not along the length of the limb, the surface of the muscle was agglutinated, while all the rest remained unagglutinated.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: μύλην, κατὰ πλάτος ὀνομαζομένης ἀγωγῆς, ἀποδείρας, εἰς αὐτὸ τὸ σαρκῶδες

For having observed 601 one of the gladiators called 'horsemen' with a transverse division, reaching to a great depth at the front and lower part of the thigh, and having seen the lips of the wound, the one drawn up above and the other drawn down below, as if towards the kneecap, I ventured, departing from what is named the 'broad' method, to bring the separated parts of the muscles together to one another with sutures. Taking care, then, not to stitch the tendons together with them, I first stripped those away, and in this way I used the sutures through a great depth, knowing that there is no danger as regards the fleshy part itself of the muscles, but that the danger arises from the tendons; and I had not seen any of my teachers doing even this. For some of them, in such woundings, sutured only the lips of the skin, while others, venturing to suture the muscles themselves at their fleshy parts, made the bringing-together superficially, so that if ever the wound had come about penetrating to a considerable depth, not along the length of the limb, the superficial part of the muscle would become agglutinated, but all the rest would remain unagglutinated.

K13.601-602K. XIII 601–602, ch. 3.2 difficulty mid (4.86/100; thin 1, absent 6) (a) pass (b) pass 8/8 (c) pass 16/16 unit: pass

Greek (Kühn)

ἐπὶ μὲν γὰρ ﻿ 602 τῶν κατὰ τὸ μῆκος τοῦ κώλου τραυμάτων ἡ ἐπίδεσις ἱκανὴ συναγαγεῖν ἀλλήλοις τὰ μέρη τοῦ τετρωμένου μυός· ἐφ’ ὧν δὲ ἐγκάρσιον γίγνεται τὸ τραῦμα, τῆς ἐπιδέσεως οὐδὲν ὠφελῆσαι δυναμένης, ἐκ μόνης τῆς ῥαφῆς τὸ σύμπαν περαίνεται. ταῦτ’ οὖν ἐὰν μὴ διὰ βάθους ποιῆταί τις, ἀκόλλητα διαμένει τὰ κατὰ τόδε μέρη τοῦ μυός. ἐπιτευχθέντος δέ μοι τοῦ ἔργου, μιγνύμενοί τινες ταὐτὸ τῶν ἀπείρων ἀνατομῆς ἁπάντων μυῶν ὑποδέρουσι τοὺς ὑποκειμένους ὑμένας, οὐκ εἰδότες ἀκινδύνως μὲν τούτους ἅμα τοῖς μυσὶ συῤῥάπτεσθαι, κινδυνωδέστατα δὲ τοὺς ὁμοίως αὐτοῖς πεπλατυσμένους τένοντας. ἀλλ’ ὅπως ἂν αὐτοὶ πλατυνθῶσιν, ὅμως ἔτι παχύτεροί τε τῶν ὑμένων εἰσὶ καὶ σκληρότεροι. λεπτοὺς δ’ ἱκανῶς τένοντας ὄψει κᾀν τοῖς κατ’ ἐπιγάστριον μυσὶν ἅπασι πλὴν δυοῖν τῶν ὀρθίων, οἷς αἱ τῶν λοξῶν ὑμενώδεις ἀπονευρώσεις ἐπιβέβληνται. ἄλλος δὲ τένων οὐδεὶς παρὰ τοὺς εἰρημένους ὑμενωδῶς πλατύνεται. διασώζουσι γὰρ ἅπαντες οἱ πλατυνόμενοι πάχος ἱκανὸν, ὡς μηδένα λαθεῖν.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: μιγνύμενοί τινες ταὐτὸ, ὑποδέρουσι

For in the case 602 of wounds running along the length of the limb, bandaging is sufficient to bring the parts of the wounded muscle together with one another; but in those in which the wound is transverse, since bandaging is able to help not at all, the whole thing is accomplished by the suture alone. If, then, one does not make these sutures through the depth, the parts of the muscle in this region remain unagglutinated. But after I had succeeded in this operation, certain persons among those inexperienced in the anatomy of all the muscles, imitating the same procedure (μιγνύμενοί τινες ταὐτὸ), flay away from beneath the underlying membranes, not knowing that these may be sutured together with the muscles without danger, whereas the tendons that have been flattened in a manner similar to them are sutured only with the greatest danger. But however much these may be flattened, nonetheless they are still both thicker and harder than the membranes. You will see quite thin tendons also in all the muscles of the epigastrium except the two straight ones, upon which the membranous aponeuroses of the oblique muscles are laid. But no other tendon besides those mentioned is flattened in a membranous fashion; for all those that are flattened preserve a considerable thickness, so as to escape no one's notice.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: μιγνύμενοι (probably corrupt for μιμούμενοι, 'imitating'; rendered so), κατὰ τόδε (rendered 'in this region'; possibly corrupt, e.g. for κατὰ βάθος), ταὐτὸ τῶν ἀπείρων ἀνατομῆς ἁπάντων μυῶν (construction of ἁπάντων μυῶν uncertain)

For in the case of 602 wounds along the length of the limb, bandaging is sufficient to bring the parts of the wounded muscle together with one another; but in those where the wound is transverse, since bandaging can be of no help, the whole thing is accomplished by suture alone. If, then, one does not make these [sutures] through the depth, the parts of the muscle in this region remain unagglutinated. When I had succeeded in this operation, certain people inexperienced in anatomy, imitating the same thing, flay off from beneath all muscles the underlying membranes, not knowing that these may be sutured together with the muscles without danger, whereas the tendons that are flattened out like them are sutured with the greatest danger. Yet however much these may be flattened, they are nevertheless still thicker and harder than the membranes. You will see quite thin tendons also in all the muscles of the epigastrium except the two straight ones, upon which the membranous aponeuroses of the oblique ones are laid. No other tendon besides those mentioned is flattened in a membranous manner; for all those that are flattened retain a considerable thickness, so that they escape no one's notice.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: αὐτῆς

The sympathy, at any rate, of the parts lying above, as far as the very origin of the 603 nerves, occurs no less in the muscles at the end of the thigh, in those that insert into the elbow of the forearm, and in those that go into the heel. In both of these, then, the tendons are flattened, but while preserving a considerable thickness; and in the case of the heel still more so: the tendon descending from the calf appears both robust and thick, as being composed of three aponeuroses. This too had escaped the notice of the anatomists older than I, as had many other things concerning the muscles as a whole, and still much more concerning the veins, arteries and nerves. And many of my companions by now know all these things and demonstrate them to their pupils, when my anatomical books are read.

The sympathetic affection (sympatheia) of the overlying parts, however, up to the origin of its 603 nerves, occurs no less in the muscles at the end of the thigh and in those that insert into the elbow of the forearm, and in those that insert into the heel. In each of these the tendons do become broadened, but while preserving a considerable thickness; and at the heel even more so: the tendon that descends from the calf (gastroknēmia) appears both robust and thick, as being composed of three aponeuroses. This too had escaped the notice of the anatomists older than me, as had many other things concerning the muscles as wholes, and still far more concerning veins, arteries and nerves. Rendering A (fable-5-1)

I mentioned the things that appear from dissection — which, as someone might suppose, have nothing in common with the treatise on the composition of drugs — because I feared that those who err through such ignorance might either regard the whole method of treatment with suspicion or strip off the membranes to no purpose, being wary of the harm that would arise from the things which they falsely suspect; just as certain others, not 604 knowing how to recognise the ligaments, regard them with the same suspicion as nerves and tendons when they are wounded, although they are free of danger whatever they may suffer, since they are insensible and have their origin from bones, not from muscles, as the tendons do, or from the brain and spinal marrow, as the nerves do. Very well, then, have many of the most esteemed physicians spoken, as on many other matters, so also on these: that the parts of medicine have need of one another — I mean the surgical, the pharmaceutical and the dietetic — and most of all the part concerned with surgery has need of both the others. If, therefore, you have often seen in apes the position and size of each tendon and nerve, you will remember accurately, should you ever have the opportunity of dissecting a human body, to find each one quickly just as you had observed it. But if you are wholly unpractised you would derive no benefit from such an opportunity, just as the physicians in the German war, although they had license to dissect the bodies of barbarians, did not learn anything more than what cooks know.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: μνήσῃ, εὑρεῖν, ἀγωγὴν, μάγειροι

I have mentioned these things that become evident from dissection, though, as one might suppose, they have nothing in common with a treatise on the composition of drugs, because I feared that people, going wrong through ignorance of this kind, would either regard the whole method of treatment with suspicion or needlessly strip off the membranes, guarding against a harm which they falsely suppose will come from them. But if you are wholly unpractised, you would gain no benefit from such an opportunity, just as the physicians in the German war, who had licence to dissect the bodies of barbarians, learned nothing more than cooks know.

For the Empiricists' anatomy by chance encounter is a long piece of nonsense, like 605 goats being pricked with sharp rushes. And still more nonsensical than this is learning through the anatomical treatises, which resembles those called, according to the proverb, 'helmsmen from a book'. For when those who have clearly seen the nerves in the body and their tendons displayed by a teacher, unless they see them a second and a third time and many times in succession, do not remember accurately the place in which they lie, hardly could anyone be able to learn them by reading. At any rate, the best of the Empiricists of our time, who had read all the books of the anatomists, when a child at school had once made a stab with a stylus into the inner region of the upper arm, above its middle parts, applied one of the plaster drugs of which he had recently had experience as having cured a wound of a nerve, and seeing the child under it free of inflammation and free of pain, thought that it had been completely cured; but suddenly, on the seventh day, it died in convulsions.

So when he related to me what had happened as something hard to account for causally and perplexing, and I asked him to read out the written formula of the drug's 606 composition, he readily did this, and I at once said to him the following: 'When you were just now narrating to me what befell the freshly wounded boy, did you add anything more to your narration than that you had recently had experience of the drug having wonderfully cured a freshly wounded boy?' And when he admitted that he had added nothing else to his account, 'Then listen again,' I said, 'to me telling you what has happened. The wound of that which struck the nerve, which you say was cured by this drug, was a considerable one; and with confidence go on using it again on wounds of this kind. But punctures of nerves that have a narrow division of the skin cannot be treated by a drug of this sort, just as neither can the drug that treats the punctures be applied to the nerve itself when bare. When, then, he related to me what had happened as something whose cause was hard to assign and baffling, I asked him to read me the written recipe of the drug's 606 composition. But punctures of nerves that have a narrow division of the skin cannot be cured by such a drug, just as the drug that cures punctures cannot cure them either when it is applied to the bare nerve itself. For that drug is much hotter and more biting than a bare nerve could endure, given the biting and inflaming it produces."

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: δακκῶδες

Where, however, skin and a membrane upon it, and perhaps flesh as well, lie in front, the biting drug, provided that it is also fine-parted, produces the same treatment with respect to the nerve as your drug does 607 when it touches only the bare affected nerve itself. But one must make it a matter of the highest priority that the orifice of the narrow punctures be kept open, so that the hot drug applied from outside, being attractive of what lies in the depth, may vaporise through it the moisture around the affected nerve; so that in such cases it is fitting also to enlarge by incision the mouth of the puncture, with two incisions through the skin cutting one another at right angles. These things, then, I said, and many others of what has been stated by us earlier, to the man who had erred so greatly in the treatment of the nerve-wounded child that, because of the faulty management, it was seized with spasm and died. It is clear, therefore, that the nature of the punctured part too will indicate that the puncture is at one time free of danger, at another suspect, when we see a nerve lying beneath, as in the case of the child punctured in the upper arm.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

For it is not possible to explain in words the place at which the nerves that pass through the armpit and arrive at the whole arm mount upon the upper arm; and here too one may marvel at the contentiousness of the Empiricists, who in all other matters 608 assert that the person who has often seen any one of the works belonging to medicine imitates it better than if he happened to have seen it only a few times, and likewise that the person who does anything whatever performs it better if he has done it many times, yet that the things which come to knowledge through dissection, alone of all, can be known with certainty by someone who has merely read the account of the anatomical men—when in fact, even among those who have seen the parts of the body demonstrated by a teacher, there is no one who is able, having seen them once or twice, to remember them accurately, but one must see them many times. And not only are there large nerves travelling through the depth in many parts of the body, but there are also, as it were, certain roots of those nerves which are distributed to the skin alone; and those who have been punctured in these, if they are not properly treated, incur a sympathetic affection of the large underlying nerves from which these grow out—matters about which I wrote accurately in the Anatomical Procedures, since they had been omitted by all before me.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: σκίμποδος

And if one has not seen these roots many times in apes, it is impossible for him, even should he at some time have a human body at his disposal for dissection, to discover them accurately 609 — just as, far more so, in the case of dissection by chance encounter, which they also call the 'viewing of wounds', it is not possible to find the place from reading alone. Nor indeed is it possible for anyone to become an eyewitness of a very great many wounds, apart from those which occur in wars; and even in the case of these no one, neglecting the task of treating the wounded man as quickly as possible, investigates where nerves underlie the wounded parts, and where arteries and veins. Accordingly, in the treatments of such affections those who are inexperienced in what appears from dissection are seen to resemble blind men walking with a staff, and for this reason to err most gravely. But whoever among them has combined into one both the experience of the parts, known from dissection, and the method for the composition and use of drugs, will always treat those wounded in the nerves, as we do, without any danger, so that neither does any of the more sovereign parts suffer in sympathy nor does the wounded or bruised nerve become inflamed; and where these do not happen, far more surely neither putrefaction nor crippling nor spasm nor death follows.

And where this is so, much more will neither putrefaction nor crippling nor spasm nor death follow.

Greek (Kühn)

610 [Αἱ διὰ τῶν μεταλλικῶν φαρμάκων. συνθέσεις πρὸς νευροτρώτους.] Τῶν μεταλλικῶν φαρμάκων τὰ μὲν στύφοντα φευκτέον ἐστὶ καὶ μάλισθ’ ὅταν ἐπὶ νύγματος ἡ χρῆσις γίγνηται τοῦ συντεθέντος φαρμάκου, τὰ δὲ ῥυπτικὴν ἔχοντα δύναμιν ὥσπερ ἡ λεπὶς τοῦ χαλκοῦ χρήσιμα. παχυμερῆ δ’ ὄντα πάντα τὰ μεταλλικὰ, πολὺ γὰρ ἐν ἑαυτοῖς ἔχει τῆς γεώδους οὐσίας, χρῄζει τινὸς ἐπιτεχνήσεως ἐν τῷ σκευάζεσθαι πρὸς τὸ λεπτομερέστερα γενέσθαι. ταύτην οὖν ἐπενόησα δι’ ὄξους δριμυτάτου ποιεῖσθαι, καὶ μάλιστα ἐξ οἴνου μὴ αὐστηροῦ τὴν γένεσιν ἐσχηκότος, κᾂν ἐκ μὴ τοιούτου δὲ γεγονὸς ᾖ, χρόνου δὲ ὑπάρχῃ συχνοῦ, χρηστέον αὐτῷ. πρόσεχε δὲ ἀκριβῶς ἐν τῇ γεύσει μή τί σοι δόξῃ κᾂν ἐλάχιστον οἴνου ποιότητος ἐμφαίνειν. οὐ γὰρ ἔχει τὸ τοιοῦτον ὄξος ἰσχυρὰν τὴν διαβρωτικὴν καὶ τμητικὴν τῶν πλησιαζόντων δύναμιν.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

610 [The compositions by means of metallic drugs for those wounded in the nerves.] Of the metallic drugs, the astringent ones are to be avoided, and most of all whenever the use of the compounded drug takes place upon a puncture; whereas those possessing a detergent power, like scale of copper, are useful. But since all the metallic drugs are coarse-particled—for they contain much of the earthy substance in themselves—they require some artifice in their preparation in order to become more fine-particled. This, then, I devised to accomplish by means of the most acrid vinegar, and above all one that has had its origin from a wine that is not harsh; but even if it has come from a wine not of this kind, yet is of considerable age, it is to be used. Pay close attention, however, in tasting it, lest it seem to you to display even the slightest trace of the quality of wine. For such a vinegar does not possess a strong power of eroding and cutting through what comes near it.

Rendering B (opus-5-5)

610 [Compositions by means of metallic drugs. Compositions for the nerve-wounded.] Of the metallic drugs, the astringent ones are to be avoided, and especially when the compounded drug is used on a puncture; but those that have a detergent power, such as copper scale, are useful. Since all metallic substances are thick-particled (for they contain within themselves much of the earthy substance), they require some artifice in their preparation in order to become finer-particled. This, then, I devised to accomplish by means of the most acrid vinegar, especially vinegar that has had its origin from a wine that is not harsh; but even if it has come from a wine not of that kind, provided it is of considerable age, it is to be used. Pay careful attention in tasting it that it does not seem to you to show even the slightest quality of wine. For such vinegar does not have a strong power of corroding and cutting whatever comes near it.

ἐν δὲ τῷ δριμυτάτῳ καὶ ὡς οἷόν τε παλαιοτάτῳ καὶ λεπτοτάτῳ κατὰ τὴν σύστασιν ὄξει λειοῦν χρὴ τὰ μεταλλικὰ τὸ παχὺ τῆς συστάσεως παραιτούμενον, ἄμεινον δὲ ἐν ἡλίῳ θερινῷ γίγνεσθαι 611 τὴν λείωσιν τῶν φαρμάκων. ἔνια μὲν οὐδὲ κοπῆναι δεῖται τῶν μεταλλικῶν οὔτε σεισθῆναι, λυόμενα ῥᾳδίως ὑπὸ τοῦ ὄξους, ὥσπερ ἡ χαλκῖτίς τε καὶ τὸ μίσυ, τινὰ δὲ χρῄζει καὶ κοπῆναι καὶ σεισθῆναι, καθάπερ ἡ τοῦ χαλκοῦ λεπίς. ὁπότε μὲν οὖν τὸ πρῶτον ἐσκεύασα τὸ φάρμακον, ἐνέβαλον αὐτῷ καὶ ἀῤῥενικοῦ, μετὰ ταῦτα δὲ ἔδοξέ μοι περιττὸν εἶναι. καὶ γὰρ διὰ τῆς λεπίδος τοῦ χαλκοῦ καὶ τοῦ μίσυος καὶ τῆς χαλκίτεως αὐτάρκως ἐφαίνετό μοι συντεθήσεσθαι τὸ φάρμακον. ἔμιξα δὲ κατὰ τὴν πρώτην σύνθεσιν καὶ τὸν κεκαυμένον ἰὸν, ὑπώπτευσα γὰρ τὸν ἄκαυστον ὡς περαιτέρω τοῦ προσήκοντος δάκνοντα. μετὰ ταῦτα μέντοι καὶ ἄκαυστον ἔβαλον ὀλίγον, ὡς πρὸς τὴν τῶν ἄλλων ἀναλογίαν οὕτως δὲ καὶ τὴν χαλκῖτιν εἰ μὲν ὠμὴν ἐνέβαλλον, ὀλιγίστην ἔμισγον, εἰ δὲ καὶ κεκαυμένην, ἴσην τῷ μίσυϊ καὶ λεπίδι τοῦ χαλκοῦ. κάλλιον δ’ ἁπάντων τῶν σκευασθέντων ἤλπισά τε καὶ διὰ τῆς πείρας εὗρον, ἐν ᾧ πλείστη μὲν ἡ τοῦ χαλκοῦ λεπὶς ἦν, δευτέρα δὲ ἡ κεκαυμένη χαλκῖτις, εἶτα μίσυ.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἀῤῥενικοῦ, ἰὸν

But one must triturate the metallic drugs in the most acrid vinegar, as old as possible and thinnest in consistency, avoiding thickness of consistency; and it is better that the trituration of the drugs take place 611 in the summer sun. Some of the metallic substances do not even need to be pounded or sifted, since they are easily dissolved by the vinegar, as chalcitis and misy; but some require both to be pounded and sifted, like the scale of copper. Now when I first prepared the drug, I also put arsenic (arrhenikon) into it, but afterwards it seemed to me superfluous; for it appeared to me that the drug would be sufficiently composed by means of the scale of copper, the misy and the chalcitis. In the first composition I also mixed in burnt verdigris (ios), for I suspected the unburnt of biting beyond what is appropriate. Afterwards, however, I also put in a little unburnt, small in relation to the proportion of the others; and so too with chalcitis: if I put it in raw, I mixed in a very small quantity, but if burnt, a quantity equal to the misy and the scale of copper. And of all the preparations I both expected and found by experience to be best the one in which the scale of copper was in the greatest quantity, burnt chalcitis second, and then misy.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: παραιτούμενον, ἀῤῥενικοῦ, ὡς πρὸς τὴν τῶν ἄλλων ἀναλογίαν οὕτως δὲ

In the most acrid vinegar, one as old as possible and as thin as possible in consistency, one must triturate the metallic drugs, avoiding thickness of consistency; and it is better that the trituration 611 of the drugs take place in the summer sun. Some of the metallic drugs need neither to be pounded nor to be sifted, since they are readily dissolved by the vinegar, such as chalcitis and misy; but some need both to be pounded and to be sifted, as does the scale of copper. When, then, I first prepared the drug, I also put some arsenic into it; afterwards, however, this seemed to me superfluous. For it appeared to me that the drug would be compounded sufficiently from the scale of copper, the misy and the chalcitis. In the first composition I also mixed in the burnt verdigris, for I suspected that the unburnt bites more than is fitting. Afterwards, however, I also put in a little unburnt, in proportion to the others. And so too with the chalcitis: if I put it in raw, I mixed in a very small amount, but if burnt, an amount equal to the misy and to the scale of copper. The best of all those prepared, both as I had expected and as I found through experience, was that in which the scale of copper was in the greatest quantity, second the burnt chalcitis, and then misy.

λειούσθω δ’ οὖν, ὡς ἔφην, ἐν ἡλίῳ θέρους ὥρᾳ πολλαῖς ﻿ 612 ἐφεξῆς ἡμέραις ἅττ’ ἄν μίξεις φάρμακα δι’ ὄξους δριμυτάτου. ὅσῳ γὰρ ἐν πλείονι χρόνῳ λειωθῆ, τοσούτῳ γίγνεται λεπτομερέστερα καὶ διὰ τοῦτο χρησιμώτερα πρὸς τὰς στενὰς τρώσεις, ὡς ἐπί γε τῶν γεγυμνωμένων νεύρων φυλάττεσθαι χρὴ τὰ λίαν λεπτομερῆ τε καὶ διαβρωτικά. διὰ τοῦτο δὲ οἱ εἰρημένοι τροχίσκοι πᾶσι τοῖς τοιούτοις χρήσιμοι, προσειλήφασι γάρ τινα καὶ τῶν στυφόντων. ἔνδοξοι δ’ αὐτῶν εἰσὶν ὅ τε τοῦ Ἄνδρωνος καὶ ὁ τοῦ Πολυείδους καὶ ὁ τοῦ Πασίωνος. ἔστι δὲ καὶ ἄλλος τις τέταρτος καὶ πέμπτος, οἷς ἐγὼ συνήθως χρῶμαι, τούτων ἰσχυρότερος. ῥηθήσονται δ’ αὐτῶν αἱ ἀκριβεῖς συμμετρίαι προήκοντος τοῦ λόγου. νυνὶ μέντοι τοσοῦτον εἰρήσθω περὶ τῆς χρήσεως αὐτῶν, ὡς χρὴ λειωθέντας αὐτοὺς ξηροὺς μετὰ ταῦτα πάλιν ἐπιβαλόντα σίραιον οὕτως λειοῦν, ἄχρι τοῦ γλοιῶδές τε γενέσθαι τὸ φάρμακον καὶ λεῖον ὁμαλῶς. ὀνομάζουσι δὲ νῦν καὶ σίραιον τοῦτο παρ’ ἡμῖν ἅπαντες, ἕψημα τοῦ γλεύκους ἑψομένου γινόμενον. ἔστω δὲ καὶ τοῦτο μηδὲν οἰνῶδες ἔχον.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

So then, as I said, let whatever drugs you are going to mix be triturated in the sun in the summer season for many 612 successive days, in the most acrid vinegar. For the longer the time over which they are triturated, the more fine-particled they become and for this reason the more useful for narrow wounds, whereas in the case of exposed nerves at least one must guard against those that are excessively fine-particled and corrosive. For this reason the aforementioned trochisks are useful in all such cases, for they have taken on in addition some of the astringent substances as well. Renowned among them are that of Andron, that of Polyeides and that of Pasion. There is also another, a fourth and a fifth, which I habitually use, stronger than these. Their exact proportions will be stated as the discourse proceeds. For now, however, let only this much be said about their use: that one must, once they have been triturated dry, thereafter again add siraion and triturate them in this way, until the drug becomes glutinous and evenly smooth. Nowadays everyone among us also calls this siraion, the boiled-down must (hepsema) that is produced when the must is boiled. And let this too have nothing winy about it.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὡς (ὡς ἐπί γε τῶν γεγυμνωμένων νεύρων: taken as concessive 'though'), γλοιῶδες

Let whatever drugs you mix, then, as I said, be ground smooth in the sun in the summer season for many 612 successive days with the most acrid vinegar. For the longer the time over which they are ground, the finer-particled they become, and for this reason the more useful for narrow wounds; though in the case of laid-bare nerves, at least, one must guard against things that are excessively fine-particled and erosive. For this reason the troches mentioned are useful for all such cases, for they have taken in some of the astringent substances as well. Renowned among them are that of Andron, that of Polyides and that of Pasion. There is also a certain other, a fourth and a fifth, which I customarily use, stronger than these. Their exact proportions will be stated as the account proceeds. For now, however, let this much be said about their use: one must grind them dry and after that add siraeum and grind them again in this way, until the drug becomes glutinous and uniformly smooth. Everyone among us now also calls this siraeum, the boiled-down must produced when must is boiled. Let this too have nothing winy about it.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

And it becomes such when the must is neither from austere grapes, 613 but from grapes as sweet as possible, nor from what flows first, but from what flows last. If, then, the must is of this kind, one must boil it until half of what was put in at the beginning is left; but if it is not of this kind, it must be boiled down until a third is left. With boiled-down must (hepsema) of this kind one must dissolve the troches, so that, having a honey-like consistency and taken up on lint plugs, they are applied to the exposed nerves—and especially the more moderate of them, such as that of Polyeides, and indeed much more so if the body is soft-fleshed, in which case it is also safer to withdraw entirely from the use of the troches, employing other drugs, about which something has already been said before and will be said again.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: καθ’ ὃ

Such boiled must results when the must comes not from austere grapes but from grapes as sweet as possible, and not from the first running but from the last. 613 If, then, the must is of this kind, it should be boiled until half of what was put in at the beginning remains; if it is not of this kind, it must be boiled down until a third is left. With boiled-down must of this sort one should dissolve the troches, so that, having a honey-like consistency and taken up on lint pledgets, they may be applied to the exposed nerves. This holds especially for the more moderate of them, such as that of Polyeides, and all the more so if the body is soft-fleshed. In that case it is safer still to give up the use of the troches altogether and employ other drugs, about which I have already spoken and shall speak again.

K13.613-614K. XIII 613–614, ch. 3.3 difficulty low (3.21/100; thin 1, absent 6) (a) pass (b) pass 7/8 (c) pass 16/16 unit: pass

Greek (Kühn)

σκεπασθέντος δὲ τοῦ γεγυμνωμένου νεύρου τοῖς μοτοῖς ἔξωθεν ἐπιβλητέον ἕν τι τῶν πρὸς τὰς στενὰς τρώσεις ἁρμοττόντων, ὧν ἐστι τέτταρα γένη, τουτὶ μὲν ἓν τὸ διὰ τῶν μεταλλικῶν, ὑπὲρ οὗ πρόκειται λέγειν, ἕτερον δὲ τὸ διὰ τῶν ὀπῶν, ἐν οἶς ἐστι καὶ τὸ δι’ εὐφορβίου, καὶ τρίτον τὸ διὰ τῆς κόπρου τῶν περιστερῶν, καὶ τέταρτον, ὃ καλῶ τὸ πρᾷον, ᾧ καὶ πρὸς τὰ τελέως γεγυμνωμένα νεῦρα τῶν 614 ἁπαλοσάρκων σωμάτων χρῶμαι. τούτων τῶν τεττάρων ἕν τι περιβάλλειν χρὴ παντὶ τῷ πεπονθότι μορίῳ συνεπιλαμβάνοντα πολὺ καὶ τῶν ὑγιεινῶν μερῶν καὶ μάλιστα τῶν ὑπερκειμένων, οἷς καὶ τὴν κατάντλησιν ποιεῖσθαι χρὴ δὶς τῆς ἡμέρας ἢ τρὶς, ἐὰν ἐπείγῃ. καὶ μέντοι καὶ τῆς νυκτὸς ἐνίοτε τῶν μὲν μοτῶν ἐπικειμένων κατὰ τοῦ γεγυμνωμένου νεύρου, τοῦ δ’ ἐπιβεβλημένου κατ’ αὐτῶν ἔξωθεν αἰρομένου. παμπόλλης δ’ οὔσης διαφορᾶς τῶν ἀνθρωπίνων σωμάτων κατά τε τὰς ἐξ ἀρχῆς αὐτῶν κράσεις καὶ τὰς τῶν ἡλικιῶν μεταβολὰς, ἔτι τε πρὸς τούτοις τὰ ἐπιτηδεύματα τὸ συντιθέμενον φάρμακον ἀδύνατον ἅπασιν ἁρμόττειν. ἔστω τοίνυν διὰ τοῦτό σοι δύο παρεσκευασμένα, τὸ μὲν ἕτερον ἰσχυρότερον τῶν ὁμογενῶν, τὸ δὲ ἕτερον πρᾳότερον, ἃ μιγνὺς ἀλλήλοις ἐπὶ τῆς χρείας, ἐργάσῃ πάμπολλα τὰ μεταξὺ παρὰ τὸ ποσὸν τῆς μίξεως. ἐὰν γὰρ ἴσον ἑκατέρου μίξῃς, ἔσται σοι τὸ μιχθὲν ἀκριβῶς ἀμφοῖν μέσον. ἐὰν δὲ θατέρου πλέον, ἐπὶ τοσοῦτον ἀποκεχωρηκὸς τοῦ μέσου πρὸς ἐκεῖνο τῶν ἄκρων, ἐφ’ ὅσον ἡ μίξις ὑπερέσχεν.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: μοτοῖς, ἐπιτηδεύματα

Once the laid-bare nerve has been covered with the lint-plugs, one must apply on the outside one of the drugs suited to narrow wounds, of which there are four kinds: this one, the drug made of metallic substances, about which it is proposed to speak; another, the drug made of juices, among which is also the one made with euphorbium; a third, the drug made of pigeons' dung; and a fourth, which I call the mild one, which I use also for the completely laid-bare nerves of 614 soft-fleshed bodies. One of these four must be placed around the whole affected part, taking in also a good deal of the healthy parts, and especially those lying above, on which one must also perform the affusion twice a day, or three times, if it is urgent. And indeed sometimes also at night, the lint-plugs remaining in place upon the laid-bare nerve, while the drug applied over them from outside is removed. But since there is a very great difference among human bodies both in respect of their original mixtures and the changes of the ages of life, and further, in addition to these, their pursuits, it is impossible for the compounded drug to be fitting for all. Therefore, on this account, let there be two prepared for you, the one stronger among those of the same kind, the other milder; mixing these with one another at the time of use, you will produce very many intermediate drugs according to the quantity of the mixture. For if you mix an equal amount of each, the mixture will be for you exactly the mean between both; but if more of one, it will have departed from the mean towards that one of the extremes to the degree that the mixture exceeded.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: αἰρομένου, τὰ ἐπιτηδεύματα

When the exposed nerve has been covered with the pledgets, one must lay on from outside one of the remedies suited to narrow wounds. Of these there are four kinds: one is this one, the one made with metallic substances, about which it is proposed to speak; another is the one made with juices, among which is also the one with euphorbium; a third is the one made with pigeons' dung; and a fourth, which I call the mild one, which I also use for completely exposed nerves of 614 soft-fleshed bodies. One of these four must be put round the whole affected part, taking in along with it a large portion of the healthy parts as well, and especially of those lying above, on which one must also make the affusion twice a day, or three times if the case is pressing. And indeed sometimes at night as well, the pledgets remaining in place over the exposed nerve while the remedy laid on over them from outside is lifted off. Since there is a very great difference among human bodies, both in their original temperaments and in the changes of the ages, and further, in addition to these, in their habits of life, it is impossible for a compounded drug to suit everyone. For this reason, then, have two prepared, the one stronger among those of the same kind, the other milder. By mixing these with one another at the time of need, you will produce a great many intermediates according to the quantity in the mixture. For if you mix an equal amount of each, the mixture will be exactly the mean between both. But if you mix more of the one, the mixture will have departed from the mean towards that extreme by as much as that one exceeded in the mixture.

Let, then, the strongest 615 of the metallic ones be composed of these: let it have one part of raw chalcitis, two of misy, likewise raw, and three of scale of copper; and the weakest, likewise one of misy and of chalcitis, but both burnt, and four of the scale. To these, then, ground fine as I have said before, let wax be mixed: in the case of the strongest, double; in the case of the weakest, two and a half parts; and in the case of the middle one, which lies between these, two parts and a quarter. For example, in the case of the first of the three written down, which I said was the strongest, the total parts assembled from misy and chalcitis and scale come to six in number. To these, then, you will add twelve parts of wax. And in the case of the weakest, if there are six parts of the metallic substances, you would put in twelve parts of wax, and in the case of the middle one the amount between. Rendering A (fable-5-1)

you posit the one part to be one drachma—of all the ingredients, clearly—you put in as many 616 drachmas as there are parts; and if an ounce or a litra (for so it is customary among the Romans to name them), as many ounces or litras. There is among them a measure, with which they measure oil, incised with lines that divide the whole into twelve parts; and the whole measure is called by them a litra, and the twelfth part of it an ounce. The metallic substances and the wax, then, are weighed on a balance by means of a different ounce, whereas the oil is measured by the horn. Let the oil, therefore, be put into all the aforesaid drugs in proportion to the wax, exceeding it by half in all of them, so that if the wax is 8 ounces, the oil put in is 12 ounces, that is, the whole litra. And let the oil be like the Sabine, fine-particled and partaking least of astringency; and it is better if it is also old. But the oil called omotribes (of unripe olives) and that prepared with olive shoots are not suitable.

Greek (Kühn)

ἅπασι δὲ τοῖς εἰρημένοις φαρμάκοις, ὅταν ἐν τῇ θυείᾳ τὰ μεταλλικὰ πλείοσιν ἡμέραις τριφθῇ μετ’ ὄξους, ἐπεμβαλλέσθω λιβανωτὸς ὁ λευκότατός τε καὶ λιπαρὸς, ﻿ 617 εἶτα πάλιν ἅμα τοῖς μεταλλικοῖς λειούσθω τινὰς ἡμέρας, πρὶν τὴν κηρωτὴν αὐτοῖς ἐπιμίγνυσθαι. ὅσον δ’ ἂν ἐξ ἁπάντων τῶν μεταλλικῶν ἀθροίζηται πλῆθος, τούτου τὸ ἥμισυ μέρος ὁ λιβανωτὸς ἐχέτω. εἰ δὲ μαλακώτερον ἐθέλοις ποιῆσαι τὸ φάρμακον, ἐμβάλλειν πλέον ἢ κατὰ τὸ ἥμισυ, καθάπερ εἰ καὶ δριμύτερον, ἔλαττον ἢ τὸ ἥμισυ. καὶ μέντοι καὶ τηκομένῳ τῷ κηρῷ μετὰ τοῦ ἐλαίου τῶν εἰρημένων ῥητινῶν τινος ἐπιβάλλειν ἔξεστιν, εἰδότος σου δριμύτερον μὲν αὐτὸ γενέσθαι τῆς στροβιλίνης ἐμβληθείσης, μαλακώτερον δὲ τῆς τερμινθίνης, μέσον δ’ ἀμφοῖν τῆς ἐλατίνης. ἐκ δὲ τοῦ γένους τῶν ῥητινῶν ἐστι καὶ ἡ λάριξ ὀνομαζομένη τοὐπίπαν μὲν ὑγροτέρα τῶν προειρημένων οὖσα, παραπλησία δὲ κατὰ τὴν σύστασιν τῇ ὑγρᾷ πευκίνῃ, ἣν ἀντὶ τῆς τερμινθίνης πιπράσκουσεν οἱ ῥωποπῶλαι τοῖς οὐκ εἰδόσι δίακρίνειν αὐτάς. ἔστι δ’ ἡ τοιαύτη ῥητίνη καὶ τῇ ὀσμῇ καὶ τῇ γεύσει καὶ τῇ δυνάμει δριμυτέρα τῆς τερμινθίνης. ταύτῃ τε οὖν τῇ ῥητίνῃ καὶ τῇ τερμινθίνῃ παραπλησίαν ἔχει δύναμιν ἡ λάριξ ὀνομαζομένη, λεπτομερεστέρα δ’ αὐτῶν ἐστι καὶ διαφορητικωτέρα.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: πιπράσκουσεν, ῥωποπῶλαι

To all the drugs mentioned, when the metallic substances have been triturated in the mortar for several days with vinegar, let frankincense—the whitest and oily kind—be added in, 617 and then let it again be ground fine together with the metallic substances for some days, before the cerate is mixed in with them. Whatever quantity is gathered from all the metallic substances together, let the frankincense amount to half of that. But if you should wish to make the drug softer, put in more than the half, just as, if you wish it sharper, less than the half. Moreover, it is also possible to add some one of the aforementioned resins to the wax while it is being melted with the oil, you being aware that the drug becomes sharper when strobilos (pine-cone) resin is put in, softer when terebinth is, and intermediate between the two when fir resin is. Of the class of resins is also the so-called larix, which is on the whole moister than those previously mentioned, and similar in consistency to the liquid pine resin, which the dealers in sundries sell in place of terebinth to those who do not know how to distinguish them. Such a resin is sharper than terebinth in smell, in taste and in power. The so-called larix, then, has a power similar to this resin and to terebinth, but it is finer in parts than they and more discutient.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: λάριξ, ὑγρᾷ πευκίνῃ, ῥωποπῶλαι, ταύτῃ τε οὖν τῇ ῥητίνῃ καὶ τῇ τερμινθίνῃ παραπλησίαν ἔχει δύναμιν ἡ λάριξ ὀνομαζομένη (the repetition of ἡ λάριξ ὀνομαζομένη may indicate corruption)

To all the aforesaid drugs, once the metallic ingredients have been ground in the mortar with vinegar for several days, let frankincense be added, the whitest and fatty kind; 617 then let it again be levigated together with the metallic ingredients for some days, before the cerate is mixed in with them. Whatever quantity is collected from all the metallic ingredients together, let the frankincense amount to half of it. If you wish to make the drug softer, add more than half; just as, if you wish it more acrid, less than half. Moreover, to the wax as it is being melted with the oil you may also add one of the aforementioned resins, knowing that the drug becomes more acrid when the strobiline is added, softer with the terebinth, and between the two with the fir resin. Of the kind of the resins is also the so-called larch, which is on the whole more liquid than those mentioned before, but close in consistency to the liquid pine resin, which the petty dealers sell in place of the terebinth to those who do not know how to tell them apart. Such a resin is more acrid than the terebinth in smell, in taste and in potency. With this resin, then, and with the terebinth the so-called larch has a similar potency, but it is finer-particled than they are and more discutient.

Greek (Kühn)

μίξεις οὖν τῷ κηρῷ τῶν εἰρημένων ῥητινῶν 618 βουλόμενος ἐμπλαστρῶδες γενέσθαι τὸ φάρμακον ἕκτον μέρος, ὡς ἐὰν τοῦ κηροῦ βάλῃς λίτραν α΄. τῆς ῥητίνης ἐμβαλεῖς γο β΄. εἰ δ’ εἴη λίαν ὑγρὰ, καὶ μίαν ἀρκέσει μόνην μῖξαι. μὴ φροντίζων μέντοι τοῦ γενέσθαι τό φάρμακον ἐμπλαστρῶδες, οὐ πάνυ τι δεήσει τὴν ῥητίνην μιγνύναι αὐτῷ. καὶ χαλβάνης δὲ μίγνυε πάντως, ἔνθα μέντοι πρόκειται πρᾳότατον ἐργάσασθαι τὸ φάρμακον, ἴσον τῷ κατὰ τὸν λιβανωτὸν σταθμῷ. ἔνθα δ’ ἰσχυρότερον, τὸ ἥμισυ. κατὰ δὲ τὸ μέσον ἀμφοῖν, τὸ μεταξὺ τοῦ τ’ ἴσου καὶ τοῦ ἡμίσεως, οἷον εἰ τέτταρες εἶεν αἱ τοῦ λιβανωτοῦ δραχμαὶ, δύο μὲν ἐμβαλεῖς τῆς χαλβάνης ἰσχυρὸν διαμεῖναι τὸ φάρμακον βουλόμενος, πρᾷον δὲ τέσσαρας, τρεῖς δὲ μέσον. ὥσπερ γὰρ ἔχει τι μαλακὸν καὶ ἀνώδυνον, οὕτω καὶ σηπτικόν. τοῦτο δὲ ὑπάρχει καὶ τοῖς πλείστοις τῶν μαλακτικῶν. διὸ καὶ γεγυμνωμένῳ τῷ νεύρῳ προσφέροντός σου τὸ φάρμακον οὐ πάνυ τι μεμίχθαι χρὴ τὴν χαλβάνην, ἄνευ δὲ γυμνώσεως οὐ σμικρῶς ἡ μίξις αὐτῆς, εἰς ἀνωδυνίαν συντελοῦσα.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἡ μίξις αὐτῆς, εἰς ἀνωδυνίαν συντελοῦσα

You will therefore mix with the wax, if you wish the drug to become plaster-like, a sixth part of the resins 618 mentioned, so that if you put in one pound of wax, you will put in two ounces of the resin; but if it should be very liquid, it will suffice to mix in even one alone. If, however, you are not concerned that the drug become plaster-like, there will be no great need to mix the resin into it. And mix in galbanum too, by all means: where, however, it is proposed to make the drug as mild as possible, in an amount equal to the weight of the frankincense; where stronger, half of it; and for the mean between both, the amount intermediate between the equal and the half, as for instance, if the drachms of frankincense were four, you will put in two of galbanum if you wish the drug to remain strong, four if mild, and three for the middle. For just as it has something soft and pain-relieving, so too it has something septic. And this belongs also to most of the emollients. Therefore, when you are applying the drug to a nerve that has been laid bare, the galbanum should not be mixed in to any great extent; but without denudation its admixture is of no small account, contributing to freedom from pain.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: γο β΄, σηπτικόν, οὐ πάνυ τι, συντελοῦσα, νεύρῳ

If you wish the drug to become plaster-like, you will therefore mix 618 a sixth part of the aforementioned resins with the wax, so that if you put in 1 litra of wax, you will add 2 ounces of the resin. If the resin is very liquid, it will suffice to mix in only one. If, however, you are not concerned that the drug should become plaster-like, there will be no great need to mix the resin into it. Mix in galbanum too, by all means. Where the aim is to make the drug as mild as possible, add an amount equal to the weight of the frankincense; where the aim is a stronger drug, half. For the mean between the two, add the amount between the equal and the half. For example, if the drachms of frankincense are four, you will add two of galbanum if you wish the drug to remain strong, four if you wish it mild, and three if you wish it middling. For just as galbanum has something softening and anodyne, so it also has something putrefactive. This belongs also to most of the emollients. Therefore, when you apply the drug to a nerve that has been laid bare, the galbanum ought hardly to be mixed in at all. Without such laying bare, however, its admixture contributes not a little to freedom from pain.

Greek (Kühn)

καὶ τοίνυν ἔνεστοί σοι καὶ τοιοῦτον φάρμακον ἔχειν ἕτοιμον ἐπὶ τῶν εὐαισθήτων τε καὶ ἁπαλοσάρκων καὶ κακοχύμων, ἐν ᾧ πλέον 619 ἐστὶ τῆς χαλβάνης. ἅπαντα γὰρ τὰ μεταλλικὰ τραχυντικά τ’ ἐστὶ καὶ ὀδυνηρά. καὶ τῶν ὑγρῶν ὄξος. εἰκότως οὖν τὰ δι’ αὐτῶν σκευαζόμενα φάρμακα τραχέα τε φαίνεται καὶ ὀδυνηρά. καὶ διὰ τοῦτο αὐτοῖς ἐπιτήδειός ἐστιν ἥ τε δι’ ἐλαίου κατάντλησις ἥ τε μίξις τῆς χαλβάνης ὁμοίως. ἔστι δ’ οὐδὲν ἧττον χαλβάνης ἀνώδυνον τὸ λεπτομερὲς στέαρ. ἔχειν οὖν ἀξιῶ σέ τι καὶ διὰ τούτων ἐσκευασμένον ἕτερον φάρμακον, ὃ μιγνύναι δυνήσῃ τῷ διὰ τῶν μεταλλικῶν, ἐπί τε τῶν εἰρημένων σωμάτων, ὅσα τε ἄλλα τραχέος τε καὶ ὀδυνηροῦ τοῦ φαρμάκου λάβῃ ποτὲ αἴσθησιν. ἐκλέγεσθαι δὲ καὶ τούτων ὅσα λεπτομερέστερα, τοιαῦτα δ’ ἐστὶ τά τε τῶν ἀγρίων ζώων καὶ μάλιστα λέοντός τε καὶ παρδάλεως καὶ ὑαίνης καὶ ἄρκτου, τά τε τῶν πτηνῶν καὶ μάλιστα τῶν χηνῶν. εἰ δὲ μὴ παρείη τοῦτο, τῶν ἀγροίκων ἀλεκτρυόνων τε καὶ ἀλεκτορίδων. οὕτω δὲ καὶ τὸ τῶν ἀγροίκων χηνῶν στέαρ προαιρεῖσθαι. τῶν γὰρ ἐν ταῖς πόλεσι τρεφομένων ζώων, ὡς ἂν ἀργῶν κατακεκλεισμένων, ὑγρότερόν τέ ἐστι καὶ παχυμερέστερον τὸ στέαρ.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

And so it is possible for you to have ready also a drug of this sort, for use on bodies that are highly sensitive, soft-fleshed and full of bad humours, in which there is more 619 galbanum. For all the metallic substances are roughening and painful, and, among liquids, vinegar. It is reasonable, then, that the drugs prepared from them appear rough and painful; and for this reason affusion with oil is suited to them, and likewise the admixture of galbanum. Fine-particled fat, moreover, is no less pain-relieving than galbanum. I think it right, therefore, that you also have another drug, prepared from these as well, which you will be able to mix with the one made from the metallic substances, both in the case of the bodies just mentioned and in whatever other cases there is ever a sensation of the drug being rough and painful. Of these fats too one should select those that are finer in parts; such are those of wild animals, and especially of the lion, the leopard, the hyena and the bear, and those of birds, and especially of geese. If this is not at hand, that of country cocks and hens. In the same way one should also prefer the fat of country geese; for the fat of animals reared in cities, inasmuch as they are shut up in idleness, is moister and coarser in parts.

If this is not available, use the fat of country cocks and hens. In the same way, prefer also the fat of country geese. For in animals reared in the cities, being shut up idle, the fat is moister and thicker-particled.

[On the drug compounded with euphorbium.] It has been stated by me also earlier that old euphorbium falls very far short of the new. For in colour the old appears yellower and paler 621 than the new, and in consistency hard and drier. If, however, you should wish to distinguish it by tasting, you will have a diagnosis of it that is not conjectural but certain. And the heating unguent which I said one should improvise from it will show you most precisely the power of the drug. It is composed according to the following proportion: one part is of euphorbium, three of wax, and of oil twelve. The wax must be three times the euphorbium, and the oil in turn four times the wax itself. When, then, you have compounded the drug in this way, by rubbing some part with it you will be able to know clearly how great a power it has. And it becomes most evident in the bath; for people are stung strongly, by the air indeed, but still more by the water, when the euphorbium is hot and new and strong. If, then, you have euphorbium of this kind and this very unguent that has been described, apply it confidently to punctures in which you are wary that the mouth may close.

But if it is weaker, add to the weight of the euphorbium in proportion to its weakness, sometimes putting in one and a half parts, 622 sometimes two, and at times an amount equal to the wax, and even more, if it is very old. The first application, at any rate, upon one who has been punctured in the nerve or the tendon will guide you toward the second. For if the wounded man himself says that he feels a biting, and to you yourself the parts around the puncture appear hot and the puncture itself raised more than it should be and somewhat inflamed at its lips, then either you will relax the power of the drug by the admixture of more oil, or, compounding another according to the former proportion or by means of older euphorbium, you will in this way make it well-proportioned. But if the opposites of what has been said are present, you will increase the power of the drug. And if everything appears to be in due measure, you will keep it as it is. The composition, then, which has the wax triple the euphorbium is the strongest of all, provided that, as has been said, the euphorbium is fresh; that which has it fivefold is the weakest, and intermediate between both is the one which has the wax fourfold the euphorbium.

Greek (Kühn)

τὸ δὲ ἔλαιον εἰ μὲν οἷα τὰ καλούμενα πρὸς τῶν ἰατρῶν ἄκοπα χρίσματα βούλοιο ποιῆσαι, τετραπλάσιον ἐμβαλεῖς τοῦ κηροῦ· εἰ δ’ οἵαν ἐπὶ τῶν καταγμάτων 623 τὴν ὑγρὰν κηρωτὴν ἐργαζόμεθα διπλάσιον· εἰ δὲ καὶ ξηρὸς καὶ ἀλιπὴς ὁ κηρὸς εἴη, καὶ πλέον τοῦ διπλασίου. τοὐπίπαν δ’ ἀρκέσει προσθεῖναι μέρους ἑνὸς τὸ ἥμισυ τῷ διπλασίῳ, ὡς γενέσθαι τὸ πᾶν μέτρον τοῦ κηροῦ δυοῖν καὶ ἡμίσεων μερῶν. ἐμπλαστρῶδες δὲ ποιῆσαι βουληθεὶς τὸ φάρμακον τῷ κηρῷ μίξεις τὸ ἴσον ἔλαιον. ἐὰν δὲ τὸ περιέχον ᾖ σύμμετρον κατὰ θερμότητα, ὅ τε κηρὸς παλαιὸς καὶ αὐχμηρὸς τό τε περιέχον ψυχρὸν, ὀλίγῳ πλέον, ὥσπερ γε καὶ ἐὰν ὅ τε κηρὸς ᾖ λιπαρὸς τό τε περιέχον θερμὸν, ἔλαττον ὀλίγῳ. τὸ δ’ ὀλίγον τοῦτο δωδέκατόν ἐστι μέρος ἐφ’ ἑκάτερα τοῦ μέσου. καὶ γὰρ καὶ ἕνδεκα καὶ δεκατρεῖς ἐλαίου οὐγγίας ποτὲ ἔμιξα τῇ λίτρᾳ τοῦ κηροῦ κατὰ τὸ ποσὸν τῆς τε συστάσεως αὐτοῦ καὶ τῆς τοῦ περιέχοντος θερμότητός τε καὶ ψύξεως.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: τὸ πᾶν μέτρον τοῦ κηροῦ, ἐὰν δὲ τὸ περιέχον ᾖ σύμμετρον κατὰ θερμότητα

As for the oil: if you wish to make such things as the unguents called by physicians akopa, you will put in four times the amount of the wax; but if such as the liquid cerate which we prepare for fractures, 623 double; and if the wax should be both dry and fat-free, even more than double. For the most part it will suffice to add half of one part to the double, so that the whole measure in relation to the wax comes to two and a half parts. But if you wish to make the drug plaster-like, you will mix with the wax an equal amount of oil, provided the surrounding air is moderate in respect of heat; but if the wax is old and parched and the surrounding air cold, a little more, just as also, if the wax is fatty and the surrounding air hot, a little less. And this "little" is a twelfth part on either side of the mean. For indeed I have at times mixed both eleven and thirteen ounces of oil with the pound of wax, according to the degree both of its consistency and of the heat and cold of the surrounding air.

As for the oil, if you wish to make the kind of ointments that physicians call akopa (anti-fatigue ointments), you will put in four times the amount of the wax; if you wish to make the sort of moist cerate that we prepare for fractures, 623 double; and if the wax should be both dry and without fat, even more than double. In general it will suffice to add half of one part to the double amount, so that the whole measure, relative to the wax, comes to two and a half parts. If you wish to make the drug plaster-like, you will mix an equal amount of oil with the wax. If the ambient air is moderate in heat, [an equal amount]; but if the wax is old and parched and the ambient air cold, a little more; just as, if the wax is fatty and the ambient air hot, a little less. This 'little' is a twelfth part on either side of the mean. For indeed I have sometimes mixed both eleven and thirteen ounces of oil with the pound of wax, according to the degree of its consistency and of the heat and cold of the ambient air.

Greek (Kühn)

σπανίως δὲ βραχεῖ πλέον ἢ ἔλαττον· ἵνα δ’ ἐπίδεσιν ἔχῃ καὶ λιπαρότητα καὶ γλισχρότητα καὶ μὴ ψαθυρὸν ᾖ, μηδ’ εὐδιάλυτον μηδ’ εὐαπόπτωτον τοῦ χρωτὸς τὸ σκευασθὲν, ἐπέμιξα ῥητίνης αὐτῷ, μάλιστα μὲν εἰ παρείη τερμινθίνης ἀληθοῦς, εἰ δὲ μὴ, τῆς γε εὐποριστοτάτης, ἣν ὀνομάζουσι 624 φρυκτήν τε καὶ κολοφωνίαν. ἔστω δ’ ἴσος αὐτῆς ὁ σταθμὸς τῷ κηρῷ. φυλάττει γὰρ οἷς ἄν ἐμβληθῇ φαρμάκοις τὴν προϋπάρχουσαν σύστασιν, ὅταν γε καὶ αὕτη συμμέτρως ἔχῃ. πρόδηλον γὰρ ὅτι καθάπερ ἐν ἅπασι τοῖς ἄλλοις, οὕτω κᾀν τῇ ῥητίνῃ τὸ μᾶλλον καὶ ἧττόν ἐστιν. ἐὰν οὖν ποτὲ φαίνηται λιπαρωτέρα τε καὶ ὑγροτέρα, γινώσκειν χρὴ τὴν ἐκ τοῦ κηροῦ τετηκότος ἐλαίῳ γενησομένην κηρωτὴν ἑαυτῆς ὑγροτέραν ἐσομένην. ὅλως δ’ ἐπὶ νευροτρώτων, ὅταν ἁμαρτάνηταί ποτε τῆς ἀκριβοῦς τε καὶ μέσης συστάσεως τῶν ἐμπλαστρωδῶν φαρμάκων, ἐπὶ τὸ μαλακώτερον ἄμεινον ῥέπειν, εἰ καὶ μολῦνον ἔσεσθαι μέλλοι τὸ σκευασθέν. ὡσαύτως δὲ καὶ ἡ σύμμετρος τῇ συστάσει. πίττα δὲ φυλάττει τὴν τῆς κηρωτῆς σύστασιν. ἀρίστη δ’, ὡς ἔφην, εἰς τὴν τοιαύτην μίξιν ἡ λιπαρὰ, καθάπερ γε καὶ ἡ ῥητίνη. ἐὰν δ’ ὑγρὰν ἐμβάλλῃς ῥητίνην, ὁποία κατὰ τὴν σύστασίν ἐστιν ἡ τερμινθίνη, τρίτον αὐτῆς μέρος εἶναι τοῦ κηροῦ προσήκει, καὶ αὐτῆς δὲ τῆς τερμινθίνης ἡ νέα πολὺ τῆς παλαιᾶς ἐστιν ὑγροτέρα.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἐπίδεσιν, μολῦνον, ὡσαύτως δὲ καὶ ἡ σύμμετρος τῇ συστάσει

And rarely a little more or less. But in order that the preparation may have binding quality (ἐπίδεσιν) and fattiness and viscosity, and not be crumbly, nor easily dissolved, nor easily falling off the skin, I mixed into it some resin, preferably, if it were available, genuine terebinth, and if not, at least the most readily procurable kind, which they call 624 'roasted' (phrykte) and colophony. Let its weight be equal to the wax. For it preserves the pre-existing consistency of whatever drugs it is put into, provided that it too is in a well-proportioned condition. For it is evident that, just as in all other things, so too in resin there is the more and the less. If, then, it ever appears fattier and moister, one must recognise that the cerate which will result from the wax melted in oil will be moister than itself [would otherwise be]. In general, in those wounded in the nerves, whenever one ever misses the exact and middle consistency of the plaster-like drugs, it is better to incline towards the softer, even if the preparation is going to be smearing (μολῦνον). And likewise also that which is well-proportioned in consistency. And pitch preserves the consistency of the cerate. The best for such a mixture, as I said, is the fatty kind, just as indeed is also the [fatty] resin. But if you put in a liquid resin, such as terebinth is in its consistency, it is fitting that it be a third part of the wax; and of terebinth itself the fresh is much moister than the old.

But rarely a little more or less than that. And so that the preparation should have adhesion, oiliness and viscosity, and should not be friable, nor easily dissolved, nor apt to fall away from the skin, I mixed resin into it: preferably genuine terebinth, if it was to hand, and if not, at least the most readily obtainable kind, which they call 624 roasted and Colophonian. Let its weight be equal to that of the wax. For it preserves, in whatever drugs it is put into, the consistency they already had, provided that it too is of moderate consistency. For it is evident that, as in all other things, so too in resin there is the more and the less. If, then, it ever appears oilier and moister, one must recognise that the cerate that will be made from wax melted in oil will be moister than it would otherwise be. In general, in the case of the nerve-wounded, whenever one misses the exact and middle consistency of plaster-like drugs, it is better to lean toward the softer, even if the preparation is going to be one that smears. And likewise also the one that is moderate in consistency. Pitch preserves the consistency of the cerate. As I said, the best for such a mixture is the oily kind, just as with resin too. If you put in a liquid resin, such as terebinth is in consistency, its amount should properly be a third part of the wax; and of terebinth itself, the fresh is much moister than the old.

Greek (Kühn)

μέμνησθε τοίνυν ὅπερ ἐπὶ κηροῦ καὶ ῥητίνης ἠκούσατε, 625 τοῦτο ἐπὶ πάντων φυλάττειν. οὐ γὰρ τῶν ξηρῶν καὶ αὐχμηρῶν ἢ διὰ χρόνον ἢ διὰ τὴν ἐξ ἀρχῆς φύσιν, ἀλλὰ τῶν συμμέτρων τῇ συστάσει τοσοῦτον ἑκάστου μικτέον ἐστὶν ἐν τῇ συνθέσει τῶν φαρμάκων, ὅσον ἂν ὑπ’ ἐμοῦ γεγραμμένον εὑρίσκηται. ἔστω δὴ τὸ συμμέτρως ἔχον δυνάμεώς τε καὶ συστάσεως τοῦ προκειμένου φαρμάκου, κηροῦ μὲν καὶ ῥητίνης φρυκτῆς καὶ ἐλαίου τὸ ἴσον ἔχειν, αὐτοῦ δὲ τοῦ εὐφορβίου τὸ ἕκτον, ὡς εἶναι λίτραν μὲν ἐκείνων ἑκάστου, τοῦ δὲ εὐφορβίου δύο οὐγγίας. εἰ δὲ μαλακώτερον αὐτὸ ποιῆσαι βουληθείημεν, ἀρκεῖ τὸ ὄγδοον μέρος ἐμβάλλειν τοῦ εὐφορβίου, τουτέστιν γο α΄. καὶ ἡμίσειαν, ἐπὶ τοῦ νέου καὶ θερμοτάτου, καθότι προεῖπον εὐφορβίου τῆς συμμετρίας ταύτης ὡρισμένης, ὡς ἐάν γε παλαιότερον ᾖ, καὶ διὰ τοῦτο ἀσθενέστερον, εἴρηται πρόσθεν ὡς πλέον εἶναι προσήκει τοῦτο τῆς τῶν ἄλλων ἀναλογίας. εἰ δὲ μὴ μόνης ῥητίνης, ἀλλὰ καὶ πίττης ἐμβάλλειν ἐθελήσαιμεν, ἡμίλιτρον ἑκατέρας μίξομεν, ὡς εἶναι ἴσον τὸν σταθμὸν τῷ κηρῷ. πολὺ δὲ κάλλιόν ἐστι τὸ φάρμακον, ἐὰν ἀντὶ τῆς ῥητίνης τε καὶ πίττης πρόπολιν μίξῃ τις ἴσην τῷ κηρῷ.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

Remember, then, to observe in all cases what you have heard concerning wax and resin, 625 For it is not of things that are dry and parched, whether through time or through their nature from the beginning, but of things of moderate consistency that one must mix, in the composition of the drugs, as much of each as is found written by me. Let, then, the form of the drug before us that is moderate in both power and consistency be one that has equal amounts of wax, roasted (phrykte) resin and oil, and a sixth part of euphorbium itself, so that there is a litra of each of those, but two ounces of euphorbium. If, however, we should wish to make it softer, it suffices to put in an eighth part of euphorbium, that is, one ounce and a half—this proportion being defined, as I said before, for euphorbium that is fresh and hottest; since if it is older, and on that account weaker, it has been said earlier that this ought to be more than the proportion of the others. And if we should wish to put in not resin alone but also pitch, we shall mix in half a litra of each, so that the weight is equal to the wax. But the drug is far better if, in place of the resin and pitch, one mixes in propolis equal to the wax.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: γο α΄. καὶ ἡμίσειαν, καθότι προεῖπον εὐφορβίου τῆς συμμετρίας ταύτης ὡρισμένης

Remember, then, to observe in all cases what you heard regarding wax and resin. 625 For it is not of those that are dry and parched, whether through age or through their original nature, but of those that are moderate in consistency, that one must mix as much of each in the composition of drugs as is found written by me. Let, then, what is moderate in both potency and consistency for the drug under discussion be this: to have equal amounts of wax, of roasted resin and of oil, and of the euphorbium itself one sixth, so that there is one litra of each of those, and of the euphorbium two ounces. But if we should wish to make it softer, it suffices to put in an eighth part of the euphorbium, that is, one and a half ounces. This proportion, as I said before, has been determined for euphorbium that is fresh and hottest, since, if it is older and for that reason weaker, it has been stated earlier that it ought to be more than its proportion to the other ingredients. And if we should wish to put in not resin alone but pitch as well, we shall mix a half-litra of each, so that their weight is equal to that of the wax. But the drug is much finer if, instead of the resin and the pitch, one mixes in propolis equal to the wax.
